Hyperuniformity and non-hyperuniformity of zeros of Gaussian Weyl-Heisenberg Functions
Authors: Naomi Feldheim, Antti Haimi, Günther Koliander, José Luis Romero
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
We study zero sets of twisted stationary Gaussian random functions on the complex plane, i.e., Gaussian random functions that are stochastically invariant under the action of the Weyl-Heisenberg group. This model includes translation-invariant Gaussian entire functions (GEFs), and also many other non-analytic examples, in which case winding numbers around zeros can be either positive or negative. We investigate zero statistics both when zeros are weighted with their winding numbers (charged zero set) and when they are not (uncharged zero set). We show that the variance of the charged zero statistic always grows linearly with the radius of the observation disk (hyperuniformity). Importantly, this holds for functions with possibly non-zero means and without assuming additional symmetries such as radiality. With respect to uncharged zero statistics, we provide an example for which the variance grows with the area of the observation disk (non-hyperuniformity). This is used to show that, while the zeros of GEFs are hyperuniform, the set of their critical points fails to be so. Our work contributes to recent developments in statistical signal processing, where the time-frequency profile of a non-stationary signal embedded into noise is revealed by performing a statistical test on the zeros of its spectrogram (“silent points”). We show that empirical spectrogram zero counts enjoy moderate deviations from their ensemble averages over large observation windows (something that was previously known only for pure noise). In contrast, we also show that spectrogram maxima (“loud points”) fail to enjoy a similar property. This gives the first formal evidence for the statistical superiority of silent points over the competing feature of loud points, a fact that has been noted by practitioners. In the same vein, our second order asymptotics for spectrogram maxima show that certain heuristic proxy models used in signal processing are inaccurate at large scales.

Charged zeros and hyperuniformity

We augment each zero z of F with the attribute of charge ± 1±1, according to whether F preserves or reverses orientation around z. More precisely, we inspect the differential matrix DF of F considered as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F:\mathbb {R}^2 \rightarrow \mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document}F:R2→R2 and define1.16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \kappa _z := {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} 1 & \text{ if } \det DF(z) >0 \\ 0 & \text{ if } \det DF(z) = 0 \\ -1 & \text{ if } \det DF(z) < 0 \end{array}\right. }. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}κz:=1ifdetDF(z)>00ifdetDF(z)=0-1ifdetDF(z)<0.As we show in Lemma 3.1, almost surely, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\kappa _z: F(z)=0\} \subset \{-1,1\}$$\end{document}{κz:F(z)=0}⊂{-1,1}.

The following is our first main result.

Theorem 1.1

Let F be the GWHF (1.1) and assume (1.8), (1.9), (1.11), (1.12), (1.14), and (1.15). Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sup _{w \in \mathbb {C}} \sup _{R \ge 1} \tfrac{1}{R} \textrm{Var} \Big [ \sum _{|z-w| \le R} \kappa _z \Big ] < \infty . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}supw∈CsupR≥11RVar[∑|z-w|≤Rκz]<∞.

To compare, under the assumptions of Theorem 1.1, the expected charge to be found in a ball \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {E} [ \sum _{|z-w| \le R} \kappa _z ]$$\end{document}E[∑|z-w|≤Rκz] can be as large as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\approx R^2$$\end{document}≈R2. In fact, when F has zero mean, [30, Theorem 1.12] gives the exact expression1.17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {E} \Big [ \sum _{|z-w| \le R} \kappa _z \Big ] = \frac{1}{\pi } R^2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[∑|z-w|≤Rκz]=1πR2.Thus, Theorem 1.1 shows that the fluctuation of charge at large scales is anomalously small in comparison to Poissonian statistics, where expectation and variance grow at the same asymptotic rate as functions of the observation radius. In the jargon of statistical mechanics, we say that the point process of charged zeros is hyperuniform, or that it has non-extensive fluctuations [59, 60]. Stationary analogues of Theorem 1.1 go back to [62], albeit in a less mathematical formulation.

When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_1=0$$\end{document}F1=0 and H is radial, Theorem 1.1 follows from [30, Theorem 1.14], which also provides an asymptotic expression for the variance as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}R→∞. In this article, the variance estimate is extended to possibly non-radial twisted kernels and non-zero means. The proof in [30] depends on lengthy explicit calculations and breaks completely in the presence of a mean or in the absence of radial symmetries. Thus, new methods are needed; see Sect. 1.7.

Non-hyperuniformity of uncharged zeros

When the GWHF F is a (weighted) analytic function, all its zeros have non-negative charge due to conformality, and Theorem 1.1 expresses the well-known fact that zeros of a GEF are hyperuniform [26, 32]—albeit in the novel setting of non-zero means; see also Sect. 1.5. Our second main result concerns a non-analytic GWHF and, in a remarkable contrast to Theorem 1.1, disproves the hyperuniformity of the corresponding zero set when charges are neglected.

Theorem 1.2

Let F be the GWHF (1.1) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_1 \equiv 0$$\end{document}F1≡0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H(z) = (1-|z|^2)e^{-|{z}|^2/2}$$\end{document}H(z)=(1-|z|2)e-|z|2/2. Then there exist constants c,C>0c,C>0 such that1.18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} c R^2 \le \textrm{Var} \big [ \# \{z \in \mathbb {C}: F(z)=0, |z| \le R \} \big ] = \textrm{Var} \Big [ \sum _{|z| \le R} |\kappa _z|\Big ] \le C R^2, \qquad R \ge 1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}cR2≤Var[#{z∈C:F(z)=0,|z|≤R}]=Var[∑|z|≤R|κz|]≤CR2,R≥1.

The technique that we shall develop to prove Theorem 1.2 is very general and applies to many other twisted kernels. For example, for kernels of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H(z)=P(|z|^2) e^{-|z|^2/2}$$\end{document}H(z)=P(|z|2)e-|z|2/2, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P \in \mathbb {C}[z]$$\end{document}P∈C[z], our method gives a sufficient condition for (1.18) in terms of a finite computation with the coefficients of P. We accompany the article with a symbolic software notebook [19] which performs these computations and delivers variations of Theorem 1.2. With it, we have verified the analog of Theorem 1.2 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H(z)=L_k(|z|^2) e^{-|z|^2/2}$$\end{document}H(z)=Lk(|z|2)e-|z|2/2 with k łe 5k≤5, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_k$$\end{document}Lk denotes the Laguerre polynomial of degree k – while Theorem 1.2 corresponds to the case k=1k=1, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_1(x)=1-x$$\end{document}L1(x)=1-x. The random functions that result from choosing H to be one of the Laguerre polynomials are important in mathematical physics, as they are Gaussian eigenfunctions of the so-called planar Landau equation [2, 61], or, alternatively, random polyanalytic functions of pure type [29–31].

Applications to Gaussian entire functions

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_0$$\end{document}G0 be the zero-mean random analytic function given by (1.4). It is well-known that the zero set of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_0$$\end{document}G0 is hyperuniform:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}\big [ \# \{z \in \mathbb {C}: G_0(z)=0, \, |z| \le R \} \big ] = O(R), \qquad R \rightarrow \infty , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Var[#{z∈C:G0(z)=0,|z|≤R}]=O(R),R→∞,as follows from an explicit computation of the two-point correlation function [26, 32] which also delivers the asymptotic limit of the renormalized variance; see also [50]. As a consequence of Theorem 1.1, we derive a similar conclusion when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_0$$\end{document}G0 is supplemented with a non-trivial analytic mean \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_1$$\end{document}G1 in the so-called Bargmann-Fock spaces of entire functions with quadratic exponential growth [64].

Theorem 1.3

(Hyperuniformity of GEF zeros with mean) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G=G_0+G_1$$\end{document}G=G0+G1, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_0$$\end{document}G0 is the translation-invariant GEF (1.4) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_1:\mathbb {C}\rightarrow \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}G1:C→C is entire with1.19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sup _{z\in \mathbb {C}} |G_1(z)| e^{-\tfrac{1}{2}|{z}|^2} < \infty . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}supz∈C|G1(z)|e-12|z|2<∞.Then1.20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}\big [ \# \{z \in \mathbb {C}: G(z)=0, \, |z| \le R \} \big ] \le C R, \qquad R \ge 1, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Var[#{z∈C:G(z)=0,|z|≤R}]≤CR,R≥1,for a constant C>0C>0, which can be chosen to depend only on an upper bound for the left-hand side of (1.19).

The strong version of the hyperuniformity of GEF zeros with mean that we present does not seem to follow easily from explicit computations as in [26, 32], which rely on special symmetries destroyed by the presence of a mean.

A second set of applications concerns the covariant derivative\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \bar{\partial }^* G(z) = \bar{z} G(z) - \partial G(z) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂¯∗G(z)=z¯G(z)-∂G(z)of a zero-mean translation-invariant GEF \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G=G_0$$\end{document}G=G0. The operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\partial }^*$$\end{document}∂¯∗ is the adjoint of the Wirtinger derivative \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\partial }$$\end{document}∂¯ with respect to the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2-inner product with Gaussian weight \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e^{-|z|^2}$$\end{document}e-|z|2. In the language of complex geometry, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\partial }^* G$$\end{document}∂¯∗G is the derivative of a holomorphic section G of the standard line bundle on the plane with Gaussian metric (Hermitian Gaussian measure). The set of critical points \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\bar{\partial }^* G=0\}$$\end{document}{∂¯∗G=0} corresponding to a random section G is instrumental in the analysis of heuristic or approximate models in string theory [16]. First order statistics for the critical points of G are computed (with respect to more general metrics) in [16]. We shall look into second order statistics.

A simple computation shows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F(z)= e^{-|z|^2/2}\, \bar{\partial }^* G(z)$$\end{document}F(z)=e-|z|2/2∂¯∗G(z) is a GWHF with twisted kernel \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H(z) = (1-|z|^2)e^{-|{z}|^2/2}$$\end{document}H(z)=(1-|z|2)e-|z|2/2 [30]. Thus Theorem 1.2 can be reformulated as follows:

Theorem 1.4

(Non-hyperuniformity of critical points of GEF) The set of critical points of a zero-mean translation-invariant Gaussian entire function G satisfies\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} c R^2 \le {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}\big [ \# \{z \in \mathbb {C}: \bar{\partial }^* G(z)=0, \, |z| \le R \} \big ] \le C R^2, \qquad R \ge 1, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}cR2≤Var[#{z∈C:∂¯∗G(z)=0,|z|≤R}]≤CR2,R≥1,for adequate constants c,C>0c,C>0.

We emphasize that the covariant derivative \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\partial }^* G$$\end{document}∂¯∗G is not analytic, and the study of the second order statistics of its zeros is different from the corresponding endeavor for G [26, 54]. We illustrate the results of Theorems 1.3 and 1.4 in Fig. 1 by simulations of the index of dispersion (variance of the number of points divided by the mean number of points).Fig. 1Simulated index of dispersion (variance of the number of points divided by the mean number of points) of the zeros of the GEF \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_0$$\end{document}G0, the GEF with non-zero mean \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_0+1$$\end{document}G0+1, and its critical points in a circle of radius R. For zeros of the GEF (with or without mean), we see a decreasing index of dispersion, while for the critical points, we see that it stays approximately constant showing our proven asymptotic behavior already in a finite domain. The GEF with mean \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_0+1$$\end{document}G0+1 has an extremely low probability of having zeros close to the origin and thus no zeros were observed for small R in the simulations.

Simulated index of dispersion (variance of the number of points divided by the mean number of points) of the zeros of the GEF \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_0$$\end{document}G0, the GEF with non-zero mean \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_0+1$$\end{document}G0+1, and its critical points in a circle of radius R. For zeros of the GEF (with or without mean), we see a decreasing index of dispersion, while for the critical points, we see that it stays approximately constant showing our proven asymptotic behavior already in a finite domain. The GEF with mean \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_0+1$$\end{document}G0+1 has an extremely low probability of having zeros close to the origin and thus no zeros were observed for small R in the simulations.

The critical points of G (which are almost surely non-degenerate) can be classified according to their topological index (which is almost surely ± 1±1):\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\mathcal {N}_R^+ := \#\{ z \in \mathbb {C}: \bar{\partial }^* G(z)=0, \, |z| \le R , \textrm{Index}\big (\bar{\partial }^* G,z\big ) = 1 \}, \\&\mathcal {N}_R^- := \#\{ z \in \mathbb {C}: \bar{\partial }^* G(z)=0, \, |z| \le R , \textrm{Index}\big (\bar{\partial }^* G,z\big ) = -1 \}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}NR+:=#{z∈C:∂¯∗G(z)=0,|z|≤R,Index(∂¯∗G,z)=1},NR-:=#{z∈C:∂¯∗G(z)=0,|z|≤R,Index(∂¯∗G,z)=-1}.The corresponding first order statistics are given in [16] in the context of general metrics (see also [30, Section 6.8]). As for second order statistics, we note that the topological index of a critical point of G is exactly the charge of the GWHF \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F(z)= e^{-|z|^2/2}\, \bar{\partial }^* G(z)$$\end{document}F(z)=e-|z|2/2∂¯∗G(z) (see Sect. 9.1). As a consequence, Theorems 1.1 and 1.2 can be leveraged to prove the following companion to Theorem 1.4.

Theorem 1.5

(Non-hyperuniformity of critical points of GEF with given index) The set of critical points of a translation-invariant Gaussian entire function G satisfies1.21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} c R^2 \le {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}\big [ \mathcal {N}_R^\pm \big ] \le C R^2, \qquad R \ge 1, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}cR2≤Var[NR±]≤CR2,R≥1,for adequate constants c,C>0c,C>0.

Theorems 1.4 and 1.5 can also be formulated in terms of the weighted magnitude\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} A(z) = e^{-\tfrac{1}{2}|{z}|^2} |G(z)| \end{aligned}$$\end{document}A(z)=e-12|z|2|G(z)|and the statistics of its local extrema (local maxima, local minima and saddle points); see Sect. 9.2.

Applications in statistical signal processing

Our main motivation comes from certain recent developments in the field of signal processing. The goal is to analyze a distribution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \in \mathcal {S}'(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}f∈S′(R)—called signal—by means of its spectrogram1.22\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} S f(x,\xi ) := \left| \int _{\mathbb {R}} f(t) e^{-2\pi i t \xi } e^{-\pi (t-x)^2}\, dt \right| ^2, \qquad (x,\xi ) \in \mathbb {R}^2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Sf(x,ξ):=∫Rf(t)e-2πitξe-π(t-x)2dt2,(x,ξ)∈R2.The integral, which is to be interpreted distributionally, quantifies the influence of the frequency ξ ξ in f(t) near t=xt=x.

In realistic applications, the signal is non-stationary, which means that all frequencies ξ ξ can potentially contribute to the spectrogram, and therefore the shape of the essential support of Sf (that is, the region where most of the energy concentrates) is rather unpredictable. Nevertheless, in practice, Sf is expected to be concentrated on a thin or small-measure set which carries most of the information (time-frequency sparsity), a fact that can be computationally exploited [53].

The estimation of the essential support of Sf is traditionally done by looking into large values of Sf, a task that can be challenging if f is contaminated with significant additive noise [24]. Remarkably, it has been recently discovered that in the additive noise regime the zeros of Sf provide a rich set of landmarks from which the essential support of Sf can be effectively inferred. The intuition is that the zeros of the spectrogram of (white) noise \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}$$\end{document}W behave like charged particles, and thus form a rather rigid random pattern with predictable statistics [27], from which the presence of an underlying signal can be recognized as a salient local perturbation [21, 22]. In practice, the number of zeros of Sf is computed on reasonably sized test disks, which are classified as “meaningful" if the zero count deviates significantly from what is expected for the spectrogram of noise \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S\mathcal {W}$$\end{document}SW. The union of all meaningful disks furnishes an approximation of the essential support of Sf.2

The advantages of spectrogram zeros (“silent points") over other filtering landmarks such as local maxima (“loud points") has been the object of significant numerical investigations [47, 48, 52]. The success of zero-based spectrogram filtering depends crucially on the reliability of empirical statistics, that is, on the desirable property that zero counts computed with a signal impacted by a concrete realization of noise reflect ensemble averages if calculated on sufficiently large observation disks. As it turns out, the short-time Fourier transform (STFT)1.23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} V f(x,\xi ) = \int _{\mathbb {R}} f(t) e^{-2\pi i t \xi } e^{-\pi (t-x)^2}\, dt, \qquad (x,\xi ) \in \mathbb {R}^2, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Vf(x,ξ)=∫Rf(t)e-2πitξe-π(t-x)2dt,(x,ξ)∈R2,associated with standard complex white noise \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f=\mathcal {W}$$\end{document}f=W as input can be identified with the translation-invariant Gaussian entire function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_0$$\end{document}G0 (1.4):1.24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} e^{-\tfrac{1}{2}|{z}|^2} G_0(z) = 2^{1/4} e^{-i x \xi } \cdot V\, \mathcal {W} (x/\sqrt{\pi },-\xi /\sqrt{\pi }), \qquad z=x+i\xi , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}e-12|z|2G0(z)=21/4e-ixξ·VW(x/π,-ξ/π),z=x+iξ,see [6, 7, 33]. Hence, the zeros of the spectrogram of noise \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S \mathcal {W}=|V \mathcal {W}|^2$$\end{document}SW=|VW|2 are hyperuniform (moderate variance in comparison to its expectation at large scales). This gives partial support to the success of empirical zero statistics for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S (f+\mathcal {W})$$\end{document}S(f+W): If the observed number of zeros deviates significantly from what is expected for the spectrogram of noise \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S (\mathcal {W})$$\end{document}S(W), we can conclude with high probability that a signal is present. Furthermore, a formula for the expected number of zeros of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S (f+\mathcal {W})$$\end{document}S(f+W) is known [17, Proposition 3.4], which suggests that in parts of the time-frequency plane where the deterministic signal f has low energy it does not significantly change the expected number of zeros. However, the effect of deterministic signals on the variance of the number of spectrogram zeros remained so far unknown. Thus, the hypothesis that the disks that pass the statistical zero counting test (meaningful disks) are likely to be descriptive of the underlying signal f has not been well founded.

As an application of our main results, we contribute to the analysis of non-stationary signal processing as follows.

Theorem 1.6

(Hyperuniformity of zeros of spectrograms, and lack thereof for local maxima) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \in \mathcal {S}'(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}f∈S′(R) be a distribution with bounded spectrogram Sf and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}$$\end{document}W be standard complex white noise. Then (i)The zero set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z_{f+ \mathcal {W}}$$\end{document}Zf+W of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S(f+\mathcal {W})$$\end{document}S(f+W) is hyperuniform. More precisely 1.25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sup _{(x,\xi ) \in \mathbb {R}^2} {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[\# Z_{f+\mathcal {W}} \cap B_R(x,\xi )] \lesssim R, \qquad R \ge 1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}sup(x,ξ)∈R2Var[#Zf+W∩BR(x,ξ)]≲R,R≥1.(ii)The set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\mathcal {W}}$$\end{document}MW of local maxima of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S\,\mathcal {W}$$\end{document}SW is not hyperuniform. More precisely, 1.26\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[\# M_{\mathcal {W}} \cap B_R(0,0)] \asymp R^2, \qquad \text{ as } R \rightarrow \infty . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Var[#MW∩BR(0,0)]≍R2,asR→∞.

(i)The zero set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z_{f+ \mathcal {W}}$$\end{document}Zf+W of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S(f+\mathcal {W})$$\end{document}S(f+W) is hyperuniform. More precisely 1.25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sup _{(x,\xi ) \in \mathbb {R}^2} {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[\# Z_{f+\mathcal {W}} \cap B_R(x,\xi )] \lesssim R, \qquad R \ge 1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}sup(x,ξ)∈R2Var[#Zf+W∩BR(x,ξ)]≲R,R≥1.

The zero set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z_{f+ \mathcal {W}}$$\end{document}Zf+W of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S(f+\mathcal {W})$$\end{document}S(f+W) is hyperuniform. More precisely 1.25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sup _{(x,\xi ) \in \mathbb {R}^2} {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[\# Z_{f+\mathcal {W}} \cap B_R(x,\xi )] \lesssim R, \qquad R \ge 1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}sup(x,ξ)∈R2Var[#Zf+W∩BR(x,ξ)]≲R,R≥1.

(ii)The set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\mathcal {W}}$$\end{document}MW of local maxima of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S\,\mathcal {W}$$\end{document}SW is not hyperuniform. More precisely, 1.26\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[\# M_{\mathcal {W}} \cap B_R(0,0)] \asymp R^2, \qquad \text{ as } R \rightarrow \infty . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Var[#MW∩BR(0,0)]≍R2,asR→∞.

The set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\mathcal {W}}$$\end{document}MW of local maxima of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S\,\mathcal {W}$$\end{document}SW is not hyperuniform. More precisely, 1.26\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[\# M_{\mathcal {W}} \cap B_R(0,0)] \asymp R^2, \qquad \text{ as } R \rightarrow \infty . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Var[#MW∩BR(0,0)]≍R2,asR→∞.

Part (i) of Theorem 1.6 concerns the success of empirically computed statistics for spectrogram zeros of an arbitrary signal impacted by additive white noise and completes the heuristic explanation of the success of zero-based filtering by showing that disks that are not significant for the underlying clean signal are likely to fail the zero-counting statistical test (non-meaningful disks). Furthermore, novel tests based on spectrogram zeros that aim at specifically distinguishing between signals within a given class can be expected to perform very well due to the hyperuniformity of the zero count for each signal contaminated with noise.

Part (ii) of Theorem 1.6 provides, for the first time, statistical support for the superiority of zeros over local maxima as filtering landmarks [21, 22, 24], because it shows that statistics computed with the latter suffer from much larger fluctuations in areas of the time-frequency plane dominated by noise. Theorem 1.6 also reveals a fundamental difference between the point process of spectrogram maxima and the heuristic proxy model used so far in signal processing, which approximately describes spectrogram maxima as a perturbed lattice [23]: while perturbed lattices are hyperuniform [39, 63], spectrogram maxima turn out not to be so, and, as a consequence, certain signal processing heuristics [23] are inaccurate at large scales.

As for the class of signals f covered by Theorem 1.6, we mention that the assumption that their spectrograms are bounded is standard. Indeed, it means that f belongs to the modulation space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M^\infty (\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}M∞(R) [8, 9], which includes all distributions commonly used in signal processing. The bounded spectrogram assumption is also fair from the point of view of modeling, whereas, if we were to assume that Sf decays, the proof of (1.25) could potentially be easier, as one could ignore the influence of the mean at very large scales. (In the same vein, one can most likely extend part (ii) to signals \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f+\mathcal {W}$$\end{document}f+W where Sf is assumed to decay at infinity, but in the end those details have not seemed interesting enough for this article.)

Finally, we comment on further applications to time-frequency analysis. The STFT of a signal f is defined more generally with respect to a window function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g \in \mathcal {S}(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}g∈S(R):1.27\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} V_g f(x,y) = \int _{\mathbb {R}} f(t) \overline{g(t-x)} e^{-2\pi i t y} dt, \qquad (x,y) \in \mathbb {R}^2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Vgf(x,y)=∫Rf(t)g(t-x)¯e-2πitydt,(x,y)∈R2.The most common choice for g is the Gaussian function, which corresponds to (1.23). In practice, the same signal is often processed with multiple windows g so as to average out the bias that they introduce (multi-tapering) [24, Chapter 10]. Typical choices for g are Hermite functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{h_n: n \ge 0\}$$\end{document}{hn:n≥0} as these optimize several measures related to Heisenberg’s uncertainty principle.

For the Gaussian window \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g=h_0$$\end{document}g=h0, the STFT \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_g f$$\end{document}Vgf of a general signal f is a weighted analytic function. While this is not the case for any other choice of g (up to symmetries) [3], the STFT of a signal f impacted by standard complex white noise \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}$$\end{document}W can still be identified with a GWHF as follows:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F(x+iy) := e^{-i xy} \cdot V_g \, (f + \mathcal {W}) \big (x/\sqrt{\pi }, -y/\sqrt{\pi }\big ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F(x+iy):=e-ixy·Vg(f+W)(x/π,-y/π),see [30]. Our work shows that the zero counting statistics for the STFT with respect to a general window g may fail to share the statistical advantages found in the Gaussian window case, as it implies that the zeros of the STFT of complex white noise calculated with respect to the Hermite function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_1$$\end{document}h1 are not hyperuniform.

Theorem 1.7

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_1(t):= t e^{-\pi t^2}$$\end{document}h1(t):=te-πt2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}$$\end{document}W standard complex white noise. Then the zero set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z_{h_1, \mathcal {W}}$$\end{document}Zh1,W of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{h_1} \mathcal {W}$$\end{document}Vh1W satisfies\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[\# Z_{h_1, \mathcal {W}} \cap B_R(0,0)] \asymp R^2, \qquad \text{ as } R \rightarrow \infty . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Var[#Zh1,W∩BR(0,0)]≍R2,asR→∞.

On the bright side, we are able to show that, for a general window function g, charge statistics, corresponding to sums of winding numbers of the STFT:1.28\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu _{z} f= {{\,\textrm{sgn}\,}}\Big \{\Im \Big [ \partial _x V_g f(x,y) \cdot \overline{ \partial _y V_g f(x,y)} \,\Big ] \Big \}, \qquad z=x+iy, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μzf=sgn{ℑ[∂xVgf(x,y)·∂yVgf(x,y)¯]},z=x+iy,do exhibit moderate fluctuations at large scales in the presence of noise, and thus offer an attractive novel alternative to zero-based filtering.

Theorem 1.8

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g \in \mathcal {S}(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}g∈S(R) be non-zero, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \in M^\infty (\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}f∈M∞(R) (i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_gf \in L^\infty (\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}Vgf∈L∞(R)), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}$$\end{document}W standard complex white noise. Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}\Big [ {\sum }_{|z| \le R,\, V_g\, (f+\mathcal {W}) (z) = 0} \,\,\mu _z (f+\mathcal {W}) \Big ]\le C_g R, \qquad R \ge 1, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Var[∑|z|≤R,Vg(f+W)(z)=0μz(f+W)]≤CgR,R≥1,for a constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_g$$\end{document}Cg.

Methods and related literature

The model of twisted stationary Gaussian fields was introduced in [30], where first and second order statistics for zero sets were derived by means of Kac-Rice formulae and laborious explicit computations, and under zero-mean and strong symmetry assumptions (such as radiality). The results that we present here seem to be out of the scope of such direct methods.

To prove Theorems 1.1 and 1.2, the primary challenge lies in adapting techniques for stationary random fields to the case of twisted stationary random functions. A main insight that we systematically exploit is that in the twisted setting the differential operators \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}_1, \mathcal {D}_2$$\end{document}D1,D2 play a role that is analogous to that of the Euclidean derivatives in the classical stationary context. Besides that basic common element, the proofs of Theorems 1.1 and 1.2 are based on rather independent techniques.

To prove Theorem 1.1, we draw inspiration from [11, 13, 20], which study, respectively, the argument change of Gaussian entire functions along curves, the variance of analytic stationary random fields, and the winding of complex-valued stationary random fields. As a first step, we express the total charge of the zero set of a GWHF F using Poincaré’s index formula\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \sum _{z \in B, F(z)=0} \kappa _z= \frac{1}{2\pi i} \int _{\partial B} \frac{dF}{F}, $$\end{document}∑z∈B,F(z)=0κz=12πi∫∂BdFF,which we suitably modify to make it covariant under twisted shifts (see Lemma 3.1). This leads us to the task of analyzing correlations among quotients of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}_j F/F$$\end{document}DjF/F. While in the stationary setting this can be done by means of rather explicit formulae that go back to Kahane [38], in the twisted setting we need to derive new estimates. These necessitate arguments that are subtler than those used for related purposes in [20], which, for example, break down in the presence of a non-zero mean. Another challenge is that there is no simple description of the positive-definiteness of the twisted kernel (1.2) – in contrast to the Euclidean stationary situation, where covariance kernels are characterized by the positivity of their Fourier transforms. This leads us to indirect arguments that exploit the positive-definiteness of (1.2) to analyze the Hessian of the squared twisted kernel \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|H|^2$$\end{document}|H|2.

Our second main result, Theorem 1.2, is proved by developing a chaos expansion [37] of the zero counting statistic, a technique that goes back to [40, 55, 56] in the stationary setting, and has been successfully applied in several contexts; see, e.g., [15, 41, 51]. In the twisted stationary setting, we develop an expansion adapted to the twisted derivatives \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}_1$$\end{document}D1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}_2$$\end{document}D2. In Theorem 7.3 below, we obtain a chaos expansion for the zero statistic of a GWHF with general radial twisted kernel. With the chaos expansion at hand, we then deduce the non-hyperuniformity of the zero set in question by explicitly estimating the projection of the zero count statistic into the so-called second order chaotic subspace. The corresponding computations rely on the so-called Feynman diagram method [37], which has also been used for related purposes in, e.g., [12, 13, 57].

As a central technical step towards the chaos expansion, we prove a uniform upper bound for the two-point function at non-zero levels, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{F=u\}$$\end{document}{F=u}, (Proposition 6.2) – a conclusion that in the stationary case follows by simply inspecting the spectral measure, see, e.g., [43]. As a consequence of the uniform bound on the two-point function, we conclude in Theorem 6.4 that the first and second moments of u-level counting statistics are continuous in the level u, just as is the case in the stationary setting [40]. We view this as a result of independent interest, and note that the proof technique is general enough to be applicable beyond the setting of Gaussian Weyl-Heisenberg functions. We expect that the chaos expansion will be useful in further applications, such as proving a CLT (see [4] in the stationary setting). Although we develop the chaos expansion for uncharged zeros, we expect the same to apply with charged zeros (and to be, in fact, technically easier).

Organization

Section 2 introduces the notation and relates properties of the covariance kernel (1.2) to pointwise properties of the twisted kernel H. Section 3 briefly discusses Poincaré’s index formula and reformulates it in terms of twisted derivatives. Section 4 obtains several correlation estimates related to the terms in Poincaré’s formula, which are instrumental in studying winding numbers. Section 5 contains a proof of Theorem 1.1, in fact in a slightly more quantitative form (Theorem 5.1).

Section 6 investigates non-zero level crossings \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{F=u\}$$\end{document}{F=u} and their dependence on u. This lays the technical foundation for Sect. 7, which derives the chaos expansion of the zero counting statistic (Theorem 7.3). This is done for general radial twisted kernels H. The approach can be extended to non-radial H at the cost of additional technicalities, which in the end did not seem to merit inclusion in this article. In Sect. 8, we prove Theorem 1.2 by means of explicit computations with the so-called Feynman diagrams corresponding to the chaos expansion of the zero statistic and the particular twisted kernel \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H(z)=(1-|z|^2) e^{-|z|^2/2}$$\end{document}H(z)=(1-|z|2)e-|z|2/2. This requires certain algebraic computations with Laguerre polynomials, which are presented as an appendix (Sect. A), and can also be followed with a symbolic software notebook publicly available at [19]. The notebook also delivers the calculations that are relevant to establish analogues of Theorem 1.2 with respect to twisted kernels of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H(z) = P(|z|^2) e^{-|z|^2/2}$$\end{document}H(z)=P(|z|2)e-|z|2/2, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P \in \mathbb {C}[z]$$\end{document}P∈C[z], which are also of interest, cf. Sect. 1.4.

Finally, Sect. 9 provides detailed arguments for the applications described in Sect. 1.5 (Gaussian entire functions) and Sect. 1.6 (time-frequency analysis). In particular, Theorems 1.3, 1.4, and 1.5 are proved in Sect. 9.1, while Sect. 9.2 discusses a reformulation of these in terms of the classification of critical points of weighted entire functions; and Theorem 1.6, 1.7, and 1.8 are proved in Sect. 9.3. Sect. 10 contains auxiliary results.

Preliminaries on twisted stationarity

Notation

The indicator function of a set E is denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1_E$$\end{document}1E. We adopt the usual complex-variable notation dz= dx + i dydz=dx+idy, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\bar{z}= dx - i dy$$\end{document}dz¯=dx-idy, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|dz|= \sqrt{(dx)^2 + (dy)^2}$$\end{document}|dz|=(dx)2+(dy)2, while the differential of the Lebesgue (area) measure on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {C}$$\end{document}C is denoted dA. For short, we sometimes write dA(z, w) for dA(z)dA(w). We will write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta =\partial \bar{\partial }$$\end{document}Δ=∂∂¯, which is one quarter of the standard Laplace operator.

The Jacobian of a function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F:\mathbb {C} \rightarrow \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}F:C→C at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z \in \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}z∈C is the determinant of its differential matrix DF considered as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F:\mathbb {R}^2 \rightarrow \mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document}F:R2→R2:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\,\textrm{Jac}\,}}F(z) := \det DF(z). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}JacF(z):=detDF(z).Recall that the charge of F at a zero z is given by (1.16). The total charge of F on a domain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega \subset \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}Ω⊂C is defined as2.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \kappa _{\Omega } := \sum _{z \in \Omega , F(z)=0} \kappa _z. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}κΩ:=∑z∈Ω,F(z)=0κz.We will also make extensive use of the covariant differential operators (1.6) and the twisted shifts \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T}_{w}$$\end{document}Tw (1.3). If the operators \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}_j$$\end{document}Dj are applied to a function of more than one variable, we specify the relevant variable in a second subscript, e.g., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}_{j,z}F(w,z)$$\end{document}Dj,zF(w,z).

Covariances

In what follows, we will often need the covariance between F(z) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}_jF(z)$$\end{document}DjF(z), j=1,2j=1,2, at different points z. The following lemma expresses these in terms of twisted shifts.

Lemma 2.1

Let F be the GWHF (1.1) with twisted kernel H and assume (1.9) and (1.14). Then the following hold.2.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {E} \big [ F_0(z) \cdot \overline{F_0(w)} \big ]&= \mathcal {T}_{w}H(z) , \qquad z,w\in \mathbb {C}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[F0(z)·F0(w)¯]=TwH(z),z,w∈C.2.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {E} \big [ \mathcal {D}_jF_0(z) \cdot \overline{F_0(w)} \big ]&= \mathcal {D}_j\mathcal {T}_{w}H(z) = \mathcal {T}_w\mathcal {D}_jH(z) , \qquad z,w\in \mathbb {C},\qquad j\in \{1,2\}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[DjF0(z)·F0(w)¯]=DjTwH(z)=TwDjH(z),z,w∈C,j∈{1,2},2.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {E} \big [ \mathcal {D}_jF_0(z) \cdot \overline{\mathcal {D}_kF_0(w)} \big ]&= - \mathcal {T}_{w}\mathcal {D}_{j}\overline{\mathcal {D}_{k}}H(z) , \qquad z,w\in \mathbb {C}, \qquad j,k\in \{1,2\}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[DjF0(z)·DkF0(w)¯]=-TwDjDk¯H(z),z,w∈C,j,k∈{1,2}.

Proof

The expression (2.2) is clear from the definitions, while (2.3) follows from (2.2) and (1.7) after exchanging expectation with the operators \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}_j$$\end{document}Dj. This is allowed because the covariance of F is smooth and F is separable [5, Chapter 1]. Similarly, to prove (2.4) we can further rewrite\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {E} \big [ \mathcal {D}_jF_0(z) \cdot \overline{\mathcal {D}_kF_0(w)} \big ]&= \mathcal {D}_{j,z}\overline{\mathcal {D}_{k,w}}\mathcal {T}_{w}H(z) \\&= - \mathcal {D}_{j,z}\mathcal {T}_{w}\overline{\mathcal {D}_{k,z}}H(z) \\&= - \mathcal {T}_{w}\mathcal {D}_{j,z}\overline{\mathcal {D}_{k,z}}H(z) \\&= - \mathcal {T}_{w}\mathcal {D}_{j}\overline{\mathcal {D}_{k}}H(z) , \qquad z,w\in \mathbb {C}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[DjF0(z)·DkF0(w)¯]=Dj,zDk,w¯TwH(z)=-Dj,zTwDk,z¯H(z)=-TwDj,zDk,z¯H(z)=-TwDjDk¯H(z),z,w∈C,where we used that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\mathcal {D}_{k,w}}\mathcal {T}_{w}H(z) = -\mathcal {T}_{w}\overline{\mathcal {D}_{k,z}}H(z) $$\end{document}Dk,w¯TwH(z)=-TwDk,z¯H(z). □ □

Invertibility of the Hessian of the squared twisted kernel

We will show that our assumptions on H imply that there exists a positive constant c such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ 1-|H(z)|^2 \ge c|z|^2 $$\end{document}1-|H(z)|2≥c|z|2for z in a neighborhood of the origin. The proof requires the following two lemmas.

Lemma 2.2

Let F be the GWHF (1.1) and satisfy (1.8), ..., (1.15) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z \in \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}z∈C. Then the covariance matrix of the Gaussian vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (F(z), \mathcal {D}_1 F(z), \mathcal {D}_2F(z)\big )$$\end{document}(F(z),D1F(z),D2F(z)) is given by2.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} A=\begin{pmatrix} 1 & -\bar{\partial } H(0) & - \partial H(0) \\ \partial H(0) & -\Delta H(0)+\frac{1}{2} & -\partial ^2 H(0) \\ \bar{\partial } H(0) & -\bar{\partial }^2 H(0) & -\Delta H(0)- \frac{1}{2} \end{pmatrix}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}A=1-∂¯H(0)-∂H(0)∂H(0)-ΔH(0)+12-∂2H(0)∂¯H(0)-∂¯2H(0)-ΔH(0)-12.

Proof

By Lemma 2.1 and because \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T}_{z}G(z)=G(0)$$\end{document}TzG(z)=G(0) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G\in \{F, \mathcal {D}_j F, \mathcal {D}_j\overline{\mathcal {D}_k}F \}$$\end{document}G∈{F,DjF,DjDk¯F}, we can assume that z=0z=0. We have by (1.10), (1.11), (2.2), (2.3), and (2.4) that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {E}\big [ F_0(0) \overline{F_0(0)}\big ]&= 1,\\ \mathbb {E}\big [ \mathcal {D}_1F_0(0) \overline{F_0(0)}\big ]&=\partial H(0), \\ \mathbb {E}\big [ \mathcal {D}_2F_0(0) \overline{F_0(0)}\big ]&=\bar{\partial } H(0), \\ \mathbb {E}\big [ \mathcal {D}_1F_0(0) \overline{\mathcal {D}_1 F_0(0)}\big ] = -\mathcal {D}_1 \overline{\mathcal {D}_1}H(0)&= -(\partial - \bar{z}/2)(\bar{\partial } - z/2) H(z)_{|z=0}= -\Delta H(0) + \frac{1}{2}, \\ \mathbb {E}\big [ \mathcal {D}_2F_0(0) \overline{\mathcal {D}_2 F_0(0)}\big ] = -\mathcal {D}_2 \overline{\mathcal {D}_2}H(0)&= -(\bar{\partial }+ z/2)(\partial + \bar{z}/2) H(z)_{|z=0}= -\Delta H(0)- \frac{1}{2}, \\ \mathbb {E}\big [ \mathcal {D}_1F_0(0) \overline{\mathcal {D}_2 F_0(0)}\big ] = -\mathcal {D}_1 \overline{\mathcal {D}_2}H(0)&= -(\partial - \bar{z}/2)( \partial + \bar{z}/2) H(z)_{|z=0} = -\partial ^2 H(0). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[F0(0)F0(0)¯]=1,E[D1F0(0)F0(0)¯]=∂H(0),E[D2F0(0)F0(0)¯]=∂¯H(0),E[D1F0(0)D1F0(0)¯]=-D1D1¯H(0)=-(∂-z¯/2)(∂¯-z/2)H(z)|z=0=-ΔH(0)+12,E[D2F0(0)D2F0(0)¯]=-D2D2¯H(0)=-(∂¯+z/2)(∂+z¯/2)H(z)|z=0=-ΔH(0)-12,E[D1F0(0)D2F0(0)¯]=-D1D2¯H(0)=-(∂-z¯/2)(∂+z¯/2)H(z)|z=0=-∂2H(0).The remaining entries of A can be computed using Hermitian symmetry and (1.10). □ □

Lemma 2.3

Let F be the GWHF (1.1) and satisfy (1.8), ..., (1.15) and let A be given by (2.5). Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \det A = -\frac{1}{4} \det \begin{pmatrix} \partial ^2 |H|^2(0) & \Delta |H|^2(0) \\ \Delta |H|^2(0) & \bar{\partial }^2 |H|^2(0) \end{pmatrix} - \frac{1}{4}. $$\end{document}detA=-14det∂2|H|2(0)Δ|H|2(0)Δ|H|2(0)∂¯2|H|2(0)-14.In addition, because A is positive semi-definite, it follows that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\det \begin{pmatrix} \partial ^2 |H|^2(0) & \Delta |H|^2(0) \\ \Delta |H|^2(0) & \bar{\partial }^2 |H|^2(0) \end{pmatrix} \le -1. $$\end{document}det∂2|H|2(0)Δ|H|2(0)Δ|H|2(0)∂¯2|H|2(0)≤-1.

Proof

We will use repeatedly the relations H(0)=1H(0)=1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H(-z)=\overline{H(z)}$$\end{document}H(-z)=H(z)¯ in the process. The second identity implies for example\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial H(0)&= -\partial \bar{H}(0), \\ \partial ^2 H(0)&= \partial ^2 \bar{H}(0). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂H(0)=-∂H¯(0),∂2H(0)=∂2H¯(0).Similar relations hold for other derivatives of first and second orders.

Gaussian elimination gives\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \det A&= \det \begin{pmatrix} -\Delta H(0)+ \frac{1}{2} + \bar{\partial } H(0) \cdot \partial H(0) & - \partial ^2 H(0) + (\partial H(0))^2 \\ -\bar{\partial }^2 H(0)+ (\bar{\partial } H(0))^2 & -\Delta H(0) -\frac{1}{2} + \partial H(0) \cdot \bar{\partial } H(0) \end{pmatrix} \\&= \big (-\Delta H(0) + \bar{\partial } H(0) \cdot \partial H(0) \big )^2- \frac{1}{4} - \bigl |-\partial ^2 H(0) + (\partial H(0))^2\bigr |^2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}detA=det-ΔH(0)+12+∂¯H(0)·∂H(0)-∂2H(0)+(∂H(0))2-∂¯2H(0)+(∂¯H(0))2-ΔH(0)-12+∂H(0)·∂¯H(0)=(-ΔH(0)+∂¯H(0)·∂H(0))2-14-|-∂2H(0)+(∂H(0))2|2.Next, we will compute the determinant of the 2 × 22×2 matrix in the statement of the lemma. We have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial ^2 |H|^2(0)&= \partial (\partial H(z) \cdot \bar{H}(z)+ H(z) \cdot \partial \bar{H}(z))_{z=0} \\&=\partial ^2 H(0)-(\partial H(0))^2 - (\partial H(0))^2+ \partial ^2 H(0)= 2(\partial ^2 H(0)-(\partial H(0))^2 ), \\ \bar{\partial }^2 |H|^2(0)&= 2(\bar{\partial }^2 H(0)-(\bar{\partial } H(0))^2 ), \\ \Delta |H|^2(0)&= \bar{\partial } (\partial H(z) \cdot \bar{H}(z)+ H(z) \cdot \partial \bar{H}(z))_{z=0}= 2 (\Delta H(0)+ |\partial H(0)|^2), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂2|H|2(0)=∂(∂H(z)·H¯(z)+H(z)·∂H¯(z))z=0=∂2H(0)-(∂H(0))2-(∂H(0))2+∂2H(0)=2(∂2H(0)-(∂H(0))2),∂¯2|H|2(0)=2(∂¯2H(0)-(∂¯H(0))2),Δ|H|2(0)=∂¯(∂H(z)·H¯(z)+H(z)·∂H¯(z))z=0=2(ΔH(0)+|∂H(0)|2),so\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\partial ^2 |H|^2(0) \cdot \bar{\partial }^2 |H|^2(0) - (\Delta |H|^2(0))^2\\&\qquad = 4|\partial ^2 H(0)-(\partial H(0))^2|^2- 4(\Delta H(0)+|\partial H(0)|^2)^2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂2|H|2(0)·∂¯2|H|2(0)-(Δ|H|2(0))2=4|∂2H(0)-(∂H(0))2|2-4(ΔH(0)+|∂H(0)|2)2.We obtain the claim by comparing this expression with the expression for \det AdetA. □ □

Proposition 2.4

Let F be the GWHF (1.1) and satisfy (1.8), ..., (1.15). There exists c>0c>0 such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 1-|H(z)|^2 \ge c |z|^2, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}1-|H(z)|2≥c|z|2,for z in an adequate neighborhood of the origin.

Proof

By (1.12), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|H|^2$$\end{document}|H|2 attains a maximum at 0, and thus can be Taylor expanded near the origin as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ |H(z)|^2 = 1+ \frac{1}{2} \Big ( 2 \textrm{Re} \big [\partial ^2 |H|^2(0) z^2 \big ]+ 2 \Delta |H|^2(0)|z|^2 \Big )+ O(|z|^3), $$\end{document}|H(z)|2=1+12(2Re[∂2|H|2(0)z2]+2Δ|H|2(0)|z|2)+O(|z|3),with the quadratic form being necessarily negative semi-definite. It is enough to show that the form is in fact strictly negative definite. Suppose on the contrary that there exists z ≠ 0z≠0 such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 2 \textrm{Re} \left( \partial ^2 |H|^2(0) z^2 + \Delta |H|^2(0)|z|^2 \right) = 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}2Re∂2|H|2(0)z2+Δ|H|2(0)|z|2=0.This implies that there is a real number r such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \partial ^2 |H|^2(0) z^2 + \Delta |H|^2(0) |z|^2 = ir. $$\end{document}∂2|H|2(0)z2+Δ|H|2(0)|z|2=ir.This means\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \big |\partial ^2 |H|^2(0)\big |^2 |z|^4 = r^2 + \big |\Delta |H|^2(0) \big |^2 |z|^4, $$\end{document}|∂2|H|2(0)|2|z|4=r2+|Δ|H|2(0)|2|z|4,which implies that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \big |\partial ^2 |H|^2(0)\big |^2- \big |\Delta |H|^2(0)\big |^2 - \frac{r^2}{|z|^4} = 0. $$\end{document}|∂2|H|2(0)|2-|Δ|H|2(0)|2-r2|z|4=0.However, the left-hand side is <0<0 by Lemma 2.3, a contradiction. □ □

Proposition 2.4 and Assumptions (1.12) and (1.15) imply that2.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \inf _{z \in \mathbb {C}\setminus \{0\}} \frac{1-|H(z)|^2}{\min \{1, |z|^2\}} > 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}infz∈C\{0}1-|H(z)|2min{1,|z|2}>0.

Model-dependent constants

We say that F is a GWHF satisfying the general assumptions if F is given by (1.1) and satisfies (1.8), ..., (1.15). Many of the results below contain constants that depend on these assumptions.
