\end{aligned}$$\end{document}Lα(ξ,χ):=E[∂(z,z¯)αF(ξ)·∂(z,z¯)αF(χ)¯],ξ,χ∈K.While we do not need an explicit expression for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^\alpha $$\end{document}Lα, we see as in Lemma 2.1, that we can exchange expectation and differentiation and the regularity assumption (1.14) on H then implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^\alpha $$\end{document}Lα is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^2$$\end{document}C2 in the real sense. Thus, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi ,\chi $$\end{document}ξ,χ in the compact domain K,6.22\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&{{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[\partial _{(z,\bar{z})}^{\alpha } F(\xi )] = L^\alpha (\xi ,\xi ) \le C_K, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Var[∂(z,z¯)αF(ξ)]=Lα(ξ,ξ)≤CK,6.23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&{{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[\partial _{(z,\bar{z})}^{\alpha } F(\xi )\! - \!\partial _{(z,\bar{z})}^{\alpha } F(\chi )] \!=\! L^\alpha (\xi ,\xi ) + L^\alpha (\chi ,\chi ) - 2 \Re [L^\alpha (\xi ,\chi )] \le C_K |\xi -\chi |. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Var[∂(z,z¯)αF(ξ)-∂(z,z¯)αF(χ)]=Lα(ξ,ξ)+Lα(χ,χ)-2ℜ[Lα(ξ,χ)]≤CK|ξ-χ|.(The second estimate can be improved but this is not important for us).

We shall invoke Dudley’s inequality [5, Theorem 2.10] [1, Theorem 1.3.3], which estimates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sup _{\xi \in K}| \partial _{(z,\bar{z})}^{\alpha } F(\xi )|$$\end{document}supξ∈K|∂(z,z¯)αF(ξ)| in terms of the covering number of K with respect to the so-called canonical distance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big ({{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[\partial _{(z,\bar{z})}^{\alpha } F(\xi ) - \partial ^\alpha F(\chi )]\big )^{1/2}$$\end{document}(Var[∂(z,z¯)αF(ξ)-∂αF(χ)])1/2. By (6.23), we can bound the logarithm of the covering number in question by a constant times the logarithm of the covering number associated with the Euclidean distance. Hence, Dudley’s inequality implies that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {E}\big [\sup _{\xi \in K} |\partial _{(z,\bar{z})}^{\alpha } F(\xi )|\big ] \le C_K. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[supξ∈K|∂(z,z¯)αF(ξ)|]≤CK.Due to the Borell-TIS inequality [5, Theorem 2.9] [1, Theorem 2.1.1], the previous estimate, together with (6.22) yields\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {P}\big [ \sup _{\xi \in K} |\partial _{(z,\bar{z})}^{\alpha } F(\xi )| >t\big ] \le C_K \exp [-c_K t^2], \qquad t \ge 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}P[supξ∈K|∂(z,z¯)αF(ξ)|>t]≤CKexp[-cKt2],t≥0,which readily gives (6.21).

Step 4. We show that for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X \in \{\partial F(z), \bar{\partial } F(z), R_1(z,w), R_2(z,w), R_3(z,w)\}$$\end{document}X∈{∂F(z),∂¯F(z),R1(z,w),R2(z,w),R3(z,w)} the following estimate holds6.24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \big |\mathbb {E}\big [X\,|\, F(z)=F(w)=u] \big | \le C_K. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|E[X|F(z)=F(w)=u]|≤CK.The conditional expectation in question is the expectation of a certain Gaussian vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z_X$$\end{document}ZX—defined by Gaussian regression applied to the (zero-mean, circularly symmetric) Gaussian vector (X, F(z), F(w)) [5, Proposition 1.2]. Specifically, the conditional mean is6.25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {E}[Z_X] = \big ({{\,\textrm{Cov}\,}}[X,F(z)], {{\,\textrm{Cov}\,}}[X,F(w)]\big ) \cdot \Gamma ^{-1}(z,w) \cdot (u,u)^\intercal , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[ZX]=(Cov[X,F(z)],Cov[X,F(w)])·Γ-1(z,w)·(u,u)⊺,where we identify \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {C}^2 \sim \mathbb {C}^{1\times 2}$$\end{document}C2∼C1×2. We split the last expression as6.26\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \mathbb {E}[Z_X]&= \big ({{\,\textrm{Cov}\,}}[X,F(w)], {{\,\textrm{Cov}\,}}[X,F(w)]\big ) \cdot \Gamma ^{-1}(z,w) \cdot (u,u)^\intercal \, \\&\qquad \qquad +\big ({{\,\textrm{Cov}\,}}[X,F(z)-F(w)], 0\big ) \cdot \Gamma ^{-1}(z,w) \cdot (u,u)^\intercal . \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[ZX]=(Cov[X,F(w)],Cov[X,F(w)])·Γ-1(z,w)·(u,u)⊺+(Cov[X,F(z)-F(w)],0)·Γ-1(z,w)·(u,u)⊺.We first recall that by (6.21) and (6.20), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[X], {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[F(z)], {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[F(w)] \le C_K$$\end{document}Var[X],Var[F(z)],Var[F(w)]≤CK, and consequently also \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|{{\,\textrm{Cov}\,}}[X,F(z)]|, |{{\,\textrm{Cov}\,}}[X,F(w)]| \le C_K$$\end{document}|Cov[X,F(z)]|,|Cov[X,F(w)]|≤CK. Thus, the first term on the right-hand side of (6.26) can be estimated with Lemma 6.1 as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\left| \big ({{\,\textrm{Cov}\,}}[X,F(w)], {{\,\textrm{Cov}\,}}[X,F(w)]\big ) \cdot \Gamma ^{-1}(z,w) \cdot (u,u)^\intercal \right| \\&\qquad \le \left| {{\,\textrm{Cov}\,}}[X,F(w)] \right| \cdot |u| \cdot \left| (1,1) \cdot \Gamma ^{-1}(z,w) \cdot (1,1)^\intercal \right| \le C_K. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(Cov[X,F(w)],Cov[X,F(w)])·Γ-1(z,w)·(u,u)⊺≤Cov[X,F(w)]·|u|·(1,1)·Γ-1(z,w)·(1,1)⊺≤CK.Similarly, we use Lemma 6.1 to estimate the second term on the right-hand side of (6.26) as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\left| \big ({{\,\textrm{Cov}\,}}[X,F(z)-F(w)], 0\big ) \cdot \Gamma ^{-1}(z,w) \cdot (u,u)^\intercal \right| \\&\qquad \le \left| {{\,\textrm{Cov}\,}}[X,F(z)-F(w)]\right| \cdot |u| \cdot \left| \Gamma ^{-1}(z,w) \cdot (1,1)^\intercal \right| \\&\qquad \le {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[X]^{1/2} \cdot {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}\big [F(z)-F(w)\big ]^{1/2} \cdot |u| \cdot \left| \Gamma ^{-1}(z,w) \cdot (1,1)^\intercal \right| \le C_K, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(Cov[X,F(z)-F(w)],0)·Γ-1(z,w)·(u,u)⊺≤Cov[X,F(z)-F(w)]·|u|·Γ-1(z,w)·(1,1)⊺≤Var[X]1/2·Var[F(z)-F(w)]1/2·|u|·Γ-1(z,w)·(1,1)⊺≤CK,which finishes the proof of (6.24).

Step 5. We finally show that for every p \ge 1p≥1 and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} X \in \{\partial F(z), \bar{\partial } F(z), R_1(z,w), R_2(z,w), R_3(z,w)\} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}X∈{∂F(z),∂¯F(z),R1(z,w),R2(z,w),R3(z,w)}the following estimate holds6.27\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {E}\big [|X|^p\,|\, F(z)=F(w)=u\big ] \le C_{p,K}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[|X|p|F(z)=F(w)=u]≤Cp,K.This then can be combined with (6.18) and concludes the proof of (6.10).

First note that we want to bound the p-th absolute moment of the vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z_X$$\end{document}ZX described in Step 4. Its expectation is bounded as in (6.24) while its variance satisfies\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{Var}[Z_X] \le \textrm{Var}[X], \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Var[ZX]≤Var[X],because the conditional expectation map is an orthogonal projection. On the other hand, by (6.20) and (6.24),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{Var}[X] \lesssim \mathbb {E}[E^2] \le C_K. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Var[X]≲E[E2]≤CK.Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z_X$$\end{document}ZX is normally distributed, it follows that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {E}\big [|X|^p\,|\, F(z)=F(w)=u] = \mathbb {E}\big [|Z_X|^p] \lesssim \left| \mathbb {E}\big [Z_X]\right| ^p + \left[ \textrm{Var}\big [Z_X]\right] ^{p/2} \le C_{K,p}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[|X|p|F(z)=F(w)=u]=E[|ZX|p]≲E[ZX]p+Var[ZX]p/2≤CK,p.This proves (6.27).

Step 6. Finally, we prove the bound for the one-point function. This is significantly easier, as the probability density function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{F(z)}(u)$$\end{document}pF(z)(u) can be bounded by a constant and, thus, we only have to bound the conditional expectation in (6.8) by a constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_K$$\end{document}CK. Let z ∈ Kz∈K, write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\det DF(z) = |\partial F(z)|^2 - |\bar{\partial } F(z)|^2$$\end{document}detDF(z)=|∂F(z)|2-|∂¯F(z)|2 and estimate\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\mathbb {E}\left[ |\det DF(z)| \,\big |\, F(z)=u \right] \\&\quad \le \mathbb {E}\left[ |\partial F(z)|^2 \,\big |\, F(z)=u \right] +\mathbb {E}\left[ |\bar{\partial } F(z)|^2 \,\big |\, F(z)=u \right] \\&\quad \lesssim \left| \mathbb {E}\left[ \partial F(z)\,\big |\, F(z)=u \right] \right| ^2 + \left| \mathbb {E}\left[ \bar{\partial } F(z)\,\big |\, F(z)=u \right] \right| ^2 \\&\qquad \qquad + {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[\partial F(z)\,|\,F(z)=u] + {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[\bar{\partial } F(z)\,|\,F(z)=u] \\&\quad \le \left| \mathbb {E}\left[ \partial F(z)\,\big |\, F(z)=u \right] \right| ^2 + \left| \mathbb {E}\left[ \bar{\partial } F(z)\,\big |\, F(z)=u \right] \right| ^2\\&\qquad \qquad + {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[\partial F(z)] + {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[\bar{\partial } F(z)]. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E|detDF(z)||F(z)=u≤E|∂F(z)|2|F(z)=u+E|∂¯F(z)|2|F(z)=u≲E∂F(z)|F(z)=u2+E∂¯F(z)|F(z)=u2+Var[∂F(z)|F(z)=u]+Var[∂¯F(z)|F(z)=u]≤E∂F(z)|F(z)=u2+E∂¯F(z)|F(z)=u2+Var[∂F(z)]+Var[∂¯F(z)].As in Step 2, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[\partial F(z)] + {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[\bar{\partial } F(z)] \le C_K$$\end{document}Var[∂F(z)]+Var[∂¯F(z)]≤CK. In addition,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {E}\left[ \partial F(z)\,\big |\, F(z)=u \right] = {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[F(z)]^{-1} {{\,\textrm{Cov}\,}}[F(z), \partial F(z)] u, \\ \mathbb {E}\left[ \bar{\partial } F(z)\,\big |\, F(z)=u \right] = {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[F(z)]^{-1} {{\,\textrm{Cov}\,}}[F(z), \bar{\partial } F(z)] u. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E∂F(z)|F(z)=u=Var[F(z)]-1Cov[F(z),∂F(z)]u,E∂¯F(z)|F(z)=u=Var[F(z)]-1Cov[F(z),∂¯F(z)]u.Finally, note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[F(z)] = 1$$\end{document}Var[F(z)]=1 while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|{{\,\textrm{Cov}\,}}[F(z), \partial F(z)]| \le {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[\partial F(z)]^{1/2} \le C_K$$\end{document}|Cov[F(z),∂F(z)]|≤Var[∂F(z)]1/2≤CK and similarly \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|{{\,\textrm{Cov}\,}}[F(z), \bar{\partial } F(z)]| \le C_K$$\end{document}|Cov[F(z),∂¯F(z)]|≤CK. This shows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho (z,u) \le C_K$$\end{document}ρ(z,u)≤CK. □ □

Remark 6.3

Step 5 in the previous proof is somehow analogous to the estimate on Taylor expansions for stationary processes in [42, Proposition 1]; see also [18, Appendix A].

We can now prove that moments of level crossing statistics are continuous at the origin.

Theorem 6.4

Under the hypothesis of Proposition 6.2, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_u(K):= \#\{z\in K: F(z)=u\}$$\end{document}Nu(K):=#{z∈K:F(z)=u}. Then6.28\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\lim _{u\rightarrow 0} \mathbb {E}\big [N_u(K)\big ] = \mathbb {E}\big [N_0(K)\big ], \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limu→0E[Nu(K)]=E[N0(K)],6.29\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\lim _{u\rightarrow 0} \mathbb {E}\big [(N_u(K))^2\big ] = \mathbb {E}\big [(N_0(K))^2\big ], \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limu→0E[(Nu(K))2]=E[(N0(K))2],6.30\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\sup _{|u|\le 1} \mathbb {E} \big [(N_{u}(K))^2\big ] < \infty . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}sup|u|≤1E[(Nu(K))2]<∞.

Proof

We consider the intensity functions (6.8), (6.9). The conditional expectations defining these functions have explicit expressions [5, Proposition 1.2] in terms of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^2$$\end{document}C2-smooth vector (F(z), F(w)) and the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^6$$\end{document}C6-smooth matrix\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\,\textrm{Cov}\,}}(F(z),F(w),\partial F(z), \partial F(w), \bar{\partial } F(z),\bar{\partial } F(w)), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Cov(F(z),F(w),∂F(z),∂F(w),∂¯F(z),∂¯F(w)),which show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lim _{u\rightarrow 0}\rho (z,u)=\rho (z,0)$$\end{document}limu→0ρ(z,u)=ρ(z,0) and, for z\not = wz≠w, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lim _{u\rightarrow 0}\tau (z,w,u)=\tau (z,w,0)$$\end{document}limu→0τ(z,w,u)=τ(z,w,0).

On the other hand, the Kac-Rice formulae [5, Theorems 6.2 and 6.3] provide the representations6.31\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\mathbb {E}\big [N_u(K)\big ] = \int _K \rho (z,u) \, dA(z), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[Nu(K)]=∫Kρ(z,u)dA(z),6.32\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\mathbb {E}\big [N_u(K)^2-N_u(K)\big ] = \int _{K\times K} \tau (z,w,u) \, dA(z,w). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[Nu(K)2-Nu(K)]=∫K×Kτ(z,w,u)dA(z,w).(The formulae are applicable because F has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^2$$\end{document}C2 paths; each F(z) is a standard normal variable; for z\not = wz≠w the vector (F(z), F(w)) has non-singular covariance; while Lemma 3.1 shows that zeros are almost surely non-degenerate.)

The uniform bound in Proposition 6.2 then allows us to exchange \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {E}$$\end{document}E and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lim _{u\rightarrow 0}$$\end{document}limu→0 in (6.31) and (6.32) to obtain (6.28) and (6.29). Similarly, Proposition 6.2 provides bounds for (6.31) and (6.32) which readily implies (6.30). □ □

Remark 6.5

The analog of Theorem 6.4 for stationary processes follows by direct inspection of explicit formulae for the point intensities; see, e.g., [43].

Quadratic convergence of level crossing statistics

We can now prove that level crossing statistics are continuous in quadratic mean.

Lemma 6.6

Under the hypothesis of Proposition 6.2, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_u(K):= \#\{z\in K: F(z)=u\}$$\end{document}Nu(K):=#{z∈K:F(z)=u}. Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {E} |N_{u}(K) - N_{0}(K)|^2 \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}E|Nu(K)-N0(K)|2→0, as u → 0u→0.

Proof

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{u_k: k \ge 1\} \subset \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}{uk:k≥1}⊂C be an arbitrary sequence with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_k \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}uk→0. Let us show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {E} \big [|N_{u_k}(K) - N_{0}(K)|^2\big ] \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}E[|Nuk(K)-N0(K)|2]→0 as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}k→∞. By Theorem 6.4, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {E}\big [(N_{u_k}(K))^2\big ] \longrightarrow \mathbb {E}\big [(N_0(K))^2\big ]$$\end{document}E[(Nuk(K))2]⟶E[(N0(K))2] as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}k→∞. Hence, by the Brézis-Lieb Lemma [10, 44], it is enough to show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_{u_k}(K) \rightarrow N_0(K)$$\end{document}Nuk(K)→N0(K) almost surely. This is a standard argument [1, 5] and we just briefly sketch it.

By Lemma 3.1, the zeros of F are almost surely non-degenerate, cf. (3.1). Fix one such realization of F. Then the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{F=0\}\cap K$$\end{document}{F=0}∩K must be finite. In addition, by the inverse function theorem, around each zero z there is a neighborhood V(z) such that F:V(z) → F(V(z))F:V(z)→F(V(z)) is a homeomorphism. These neighborhoods can be assumed to be disjoint, and their union is denoted U. For all sufficiently large k, the equation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F(z)=u_k$$\end{document}F(z)=uk has a unique solution on each V(z) and therefore \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_{u_k}(K) \ge N_0(K)$$\end{document}Nuk(K)≥N0(K). On the other hand, suppose that, after passing to a subsequence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_k$$\end{document}uk, one can find solutions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F(z_k)=u_k$$\end{document}F(zk)=uk with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_k \notin U$$\end{document}zk∉U. After passing to a further subsequence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_k \rightarrow z_* \in K \setminus U$$\end{document}zk→z∗∈K\U, while, by continuity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F(z_*)=0$$\end{document}F(z∗)=0 and therefore \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_* \in U$$\end{document}z∗∈U. This contradiction concludes the proof. □ □

Chaos expansion on the complex plane

Complex Hermite polynomials

We will employ the complex Hermite polynomials \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{j,k}$$\end{document}Hj,k, defined via the generating function identity as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} e^{u z + v \bar{z} -uv} = \sum _{j,k \ge 0 } \frac{u^j v^k}{j!k!}H_{j,k}(z, \bar{z}), \qquad z \in \mathbb {C}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}euz+vz¯-uv=∑j,k≥0ujvkj!k!Hj,k(z,z¯),z∈C,or, more explicitly, by7.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_{j,k}(z,\bar{z})= \sum _{r=0}^{\min (j,k)}(-1)^r r! \left( {\begin{array}{c}j\\ r\end{array}}\right) \left( {\begin{array}{c}k\\ r\end{array}}\right) z^{j-r}\bar{z}^{k-r}, \qquad j,k \ge 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Hj,k(z,z¯)=∑r=0min(j,k)(-1)rr!jrkrzj-rz¯k-r,j,k≥0,see, e.g., [35]. Using the explicit expression, complex Hermite polynomials can be written in terms of associated Laguerre polynomials [35]. We shall only be interested in the following expression for diagonal index pairs:7.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_{k,k}(z, \bar{z}) = (-1)^k k! L_k(|z|^2), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Hk,k(z,z¯)=(-1)kk!Lk(|z|2),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_k$$\end{document}Lk is the standard Laguerre polynomial7.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} L_{k}(t)=\sum _{j=0}^{k} (-1)^j \left( {\begin{array}{c}k\\ j\end{array}}\right) \frac{t^j}{j!}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Lk(t)=∑j=0k(-1)jkjtjj!.Complex Hermite polynomials satisfy the orthogonality relation\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _\mathbb {C}H_{j,k}(z,\bar{z}) \overline{H_{l,m}(z,\bar{z})}e^{-|z|^2} \,\frac{dA(z)}{\pi } = j! k! \delta _{j=l} \delta _{k=m}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫CHj,k(z,z¯)Hl,m(z,z¯)¯e-|z|2dA(z)π=j!k!δj=lδk=m,and they form an orthogonal basis of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2\big (\mathbb {C},e^{-|z|^2}\,dA(z)/\pi \big )$$\end{document}L2(C,e-|z|2dA(z)/π). By (7.2), for any radial function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \in L^2\big (\mathbb {C},e^{-|z|^2}\,dA(z)/\pi \big )$$\end{document}f∈L2(C,e-|z|2dA(z)/π) we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f(z) = \sum _{k \ge 0} \tfrac{(-1)^k}{k!} a_k H_{k,k}(z,\bar{z}) = \sum _{k \ge 0} a_k L_k(|z|^2), \qquad z\in \mathbb {C} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f(z)=∑k≥0(-1)kk!akHk,k(z,z¯)=∑k≥0akLk(|z|2),z∈Cfor appropriate coefficients \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_k$$\end{document}ak.

Complex Hermite polynomials were discovered by [36] in the context of complex multiple Wiener integrals. They share many properties of standard Hermite polynomials [14, 28, 35]. To prove Theorem 1.2, we shall employ a so-called Wiener chaos expansion in complex Hermite polynomials, which is in many ways analogous to the more standard chaos expansion in real Hermite polynomials.

The planar chaos decomposition

Our presentation is based on Gaussian Hilbert spaces following Janson’s book [37], although we use somewhat different notation. He presents chaos decompositions in terms of Wick products rather than complex Hermite polynomials, which is just a notational difference.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F:\mathbb {C} \rightarrow \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}F:C→C be a circularly symmetric Gaussian random function with underlying probability space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Omega ,\mathbb {P})$$\end{document}(Ω,P), and consider the set of Gaussian variables7.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} S:=\{ F(z), \mathcal {D}_1F(z), \mathcal {D}_2F(z) \,:\,z \in \mathbb {C}\}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}S:={F(z),D1F(z),D2F(z):z∈C}.Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_S$$\end{document}FS be the sigma-algebra generated by S and G the completion of the linear span of S as a subspace of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(\Omega , \mathcal {F}_S)$$\end{document}L2(Ω,FS). The space G, called the Gaussian space induced by S, is a separable Hilbert space consisting of circularly symmetric complex Gaussian variables. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\xi _n \}_{n=0,1,2,\ldots }$$\end{document}{ξn}n=0,1,2,… be some orthonormal basis of G. Translated to our notation involving complex Hermite polynomials, Proposition 1.34 and Example 3.32 in [37] tell us that7.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \bigg \{ \prod _{k=0}^\infty \frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha _k! \beta _k!}} H_{\alpha _k, \beta _k}( \xi _k, \overline{\xi _k}): |\alpha |, |\beta | < \infty \bigg \} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}{∏k=0∞1αk!βk!Hαk,βk(ξk,ξk¯):|α|,|β|<∞}is an orthonormal basis of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(\Omega , \mathcal {F}_S)$$\end{document}L2(Ω,FS). Here \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha =(\alpha _k)_{k=0,1,2,\ldots }, \beta =(\beta _k)_{k=0,1,2,\ldots }$$\end{document}α=(αk)k=0,1,2,…,β=(βk)k=0,1,2,… are multi-indices, and we are using the notation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\alpha |= \sum _k \alpha _k$$\end{document}|α|=∑kαk.

The (M, N)’th chaotic subspace of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(\Omega , \mathcal {F}_S)$$\end{document}L2(Ω,FS) is defined as7.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C_{M,N}= \overline{ \textrm{span}} \bigg \{ \prod _{k=0}^\infty H_{\alpha _k, \beta _k}( \xi _k, \overline{\xi _k}): |\alpha | =M, |\beta | = N \bigg \} , \quad N,M \ge 0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}CM,N=span¯{∏k=0∞Hαk,βk(ξk,ξk¯):|α|=M,|β|=N},N,M≥0and we will denote the orthogonal projection onto \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{M,N}$$\end{document}CM,N by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_{M,N}$$\end{document}QM,N. We therefore have the following chaos decomposition:7.7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} L^2(\Omega , \mathcal {F}_S)= \bigoplus _{M,N \ge 0} C_{M,N}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L2(Ω,FS)=⨁M,N≥0CM,N.The decomposition is independent of the choice of the orthonormal basis of G; for completeness, we provide a short argument for this fact in Sect. 10.3.

Chaos decomposition of number statistics

We now look into the chaotic components of the number statistic\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} N(B) := \# \{z \in B: F(z)=0\} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}N(B):=#{z∈B:F(z)=0}associated with a GWHF F and a test set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B \subset \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}B⊂C.

This will be done by first considering certain regularized versions of N(B). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi :[0,+\infty ) \rightarrow [0,+\infty )$$\end{document}χ:[0,+∞)→[0,+∞) be smooth with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{supp}(\chi ) \subset [0,1]$$\end{document}supp(χ)⊂[0,1] and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int _0^\infty \chi (t) \, dt =1$$\end{document}∫0∞χ(t)dt=1. For ε >0ϵ>0, let7.8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \chi _\epsilon (z)= \frac{1}{\pi \epsilon ^2} \chi \left( |z|^2/\epsilon ^2\right) , \qquad z \in \mathbb {C}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}χϵ(z)=1πϵ2χ|z|2/ϵ2,z∈C.Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _\epsilon $$\end{document}χϵ is smooth, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{supp}(\chi _\epsilon ) \subset \bar{B}_\epsilon (0)$$\end{document}supp(χϵ)⊂B¯ϵ(0) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int \chi _\epsilon \,dA=1$$\end{document}∫χϵdA=1. We define the regularized variables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N^\epsilon (B)$$\end{document}Nϵ(B) by7.9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} N^\epsilon (B) = \int _B \chi _\epsilon ( F(z)) \big | |\mathcal {D}_1 F(z)|^2- |\mathcal {D}_2 F(z)|^2 \big | \,dA(z). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Nϵ(B)=∫Bχϵ(F(z))||D1F(z)|2-|D2F(z)|2|dA(z).Note that the expression involves the covariant Jacobian determinant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\mathcal {D}_1 F(z)|^2- |\mathcal {D}_2F(z)|^2$$\end{document}|D1F(z)|2-|D2F(z)|2 instead of the usual Euclidean Jacobian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\partial F(z)|^2- |\bar{\partial } F(z)|^2$$\end{document}|∂F(z)|2-|∂¯F(z)|2.

The following lemma, proved in Sect. 10.4, shows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N^\epsilon (B) \in L^2(\mathcal {F}_S)$$\end{document}Nϵ(B)∈L2(FS).

Lemma 7.1

Let F be a zero-mean GWHF satisfying the general assumptions. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi :\mathbb {C}^3\rightarrow \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}ϕ:C3→C satisfy\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |\phi (\zeta ) - \phi (\zeta ')| \le C |\zeta -\zeta '|(1+|\zeta |^s+|\zeta '|^s), \qquad \zeta ,\zeta ' \in \mathbb {C}^3, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|ϕ(ζ)-ϕ(ζ′)|≤C|ζ-ζ′|(1+|ζ|s+|ζ′|s),ζ,ζ′∈C3,for some constants C>0C>0 and s\ge 1s≥1. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B \subset \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}B⊂C be a bounded Borel set. For each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n \in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}n∈N consider a finite cover of B by almost disjoint cubes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_1, \dots Q_{L_n}$$\end{document}Q1,⋯QLn with centers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_1, \ldots , z_{L_n}$$\end{document}z1,…,zLn and diameter 1/n. Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sum _{k=1}^{L_n} \phi \big (F(z_k),\mathcal {D}_1 F(z_k), \mathcal {D}_2 F(z_k)\big ) \,\big |B \cap Q_k \big | \longrightarrow \int _B \phi \big (F(z),\mathcal {D}_1 F(z), \mathcal {D}_2 F(z)\big ) \, dA(z) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑k=1Lnϕ(F(zk),D1F(zk),D2F(zk))|B∩Qk|⟶∫Bϕ(F(z),D1F(z),D2F(z))dA(z)in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(\textrm{d}\mathbb {P})$$\end{document}L2(dP) as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}n→∞, where the right-hand side is defined realization-wise.

We now show that the regularized number statistic converges in quadratic mean.

Proposition 7.2

Let F be a zero-mean GWHF satisfying the general assumptions and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B \subset \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}B⊂C be a bounded Borel set. Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}N(B) < \infty $$\end{document}VarN(B)<∞ and7.10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} N^\epsilon (B) \rightarrow N(B) \text{ in } L^2 \text{ as } \epsilon \rightarrow 0^+. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Nϵ(B)→N(B)inL2asϵ→0+.

Proof

Step 1. Consider the Euclidean regularized statistic\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tilde{N}^\epsilon (B) = \int _B \chi _\epsilon ( F(z)) \big | |\partial F(z)|^2- |\bar{\partial } F(z)|^2 \big | \,dA(z), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}N~ϵ(B)=∫Bχϵ(F(z))||∂F(z)|2-|∂¯F(z)|2|dA(z),where, in contrast to (7.9) we use the Euclidean Jacobian. Let us show that7.11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {E} \big [\big |\tilde{N}^\epsilon (B) - N(B) \big |^2\big ] \longrightarrow 0, \text{ as } \epsilon \rightarrow 0^+. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[|N~ϵ(B)-N(B)|2]⟶0,asϵ→0+.To prove this, we take an arbitrary sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon _k \rightarrow 0^+$$\end{document}ϵk→0+ and consider the corresponding limit.

We shall invoke Lemma 6.6 and adopt its notation. By the area formula,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tilde{N}^{\epsilon _k}(B)= \int _{\mathbb {C}} \chi _{\epsilon _k}(u) N_u(B) \,dA(u) = \int _{B_1(0)} \chi _1(u) N_{\epsilon _k u}(B) \,dA(u), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}N~ϵk(B)=∫Cχϵk(u)Nu(B)dA(u)=∫B1(0)χ1(u)Nϵku(B)dA(u),see, e.g., [5, Proposition 6.1.]. Consequently,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {E} \Big [\big | \tilde{N}^{\epsilon _k}(B) - N(B)\big |^2 \Big ]&= \mathbb {E}\bigg [ \Big |\int _{B_1(0)} \chi _1(u) \big ( N_{\epsilon _k u}(B) - N_0(B) \big ) \,dA(u)\Big |^2 \bigg ] \\&\le \Vert \chi _1\Vert _2^2 \cdot \int _{B_1(0)} \mathbb {E} \Big [ \big | N_{\epsilon _k u}(B) - N_0(B) \big |^2 \Big ]\,dA(u). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[|N~ϵk(B)-N(B)|2]=E[|∫B1(0)χ1(u)(Nϵku(B)-N0(B))dA(u)|2]≤‖χ1‖22·∫B1(0)E[|Nϵku(B)-N0(B)|2]dA(u).The integrand in the last expression is bounded due to Theorem 6.4, and converges to 0 pointwise due to Lemma 6.6. Thus, the dominated convergence theorem yields (7.11).

Step 2. Let us show that7.12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {E} \big [\big |\tilde{N}^\epsilon (B) - N^\epsilon (B) \big |^2\big ] \longrightarrow 0, \text{ as } \epsilon \rightarrow 0^+. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[|N~ϵ(B)-Nϵ(B)|2]⟶0,asϵ→0+.Let us first estimate\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\big |\tilde{N}^\epsilon (B) - N^\epsilon (B) \big | \\&\quad \le \int _B \chi _{\epsilon }(F(z)) \Big | \big | |\partial F(z)|^2 - |\bar{\partial } F(z)|^2\big | - \big ||\partial F(z) - \tfrac{\bar{z}}{2} F(z)|^2 - |\bar{\partial } F(z) + \tfrac{z}{2} F(z)|^2\big | \Big |\, dA(z) \\&\quad \le \int _B \chi _{\epsilon }(F(z)) \Big ( \big | |\partial F(z)|^2 - |\partial F(z) - \tfrac{\bar{z}}{2} F(z)|^2 \big | + \big | |\bar{\partial } F(z)|^2 - |\bar{\partial } F(z) + \tfrac{z}{2} F(z)|^2 \big | \Big ) \, dA(z) \\&\quad \lesssim \int _B \chi _{\epsilon }(F(z)) |z| |F(z)| \big ( |\partial F(z)| + |\bar{\partial } F(z)| + |z| |F(z)| \big ) \, dA(z) \\&\quad \le C_B \cdot \epsilon \cdot \int _B \chi _{\epsilon }(F(z)) \Phi (z) \, dA(z), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|N~ϵ(B)-Nϵ(B)|≤∫Bχϵ(F(z))|||∂F(z)|2-|∂¯F(z)|2|-||∂F(z)-z¯2F(z)|2-|∂¯F(z)+z2F(z)|2||dA(z)≤∫Bχϵ(F(z))(||∂F(z)|2-|∂F(z)-z¯2F(z)|2|+||∂¯F(z)|2-|∂¯F(z)+z2F(z)|2|)dA(z)≲∫Bχϵ(F(z))|z||F(z)|(|∂F(z)|+|∂¯F(z)|+|z||F(z)|)dA(z)≤CB·ϵ·∫Bχϵ(F(z))Φ(z)dA(z),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi (z) = \big ( |\partial F(z)| + |\bar{\partial } F(z)| + 1 \big )$$\end{document}Φ(z)=(|∂F(z)|+|∂¯F(z)|+1).

Let p ∈ (1,2)p∈(1,2), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p' \in (2,\infty )$$\end{document}p′∈(2,∞) its Hölder conjugate, 1/p+1/p'=11/p+1/p′=1, and select q ∈ (1,2)q∈(1,2) with pq <2pq<2. Our assumptions on F imply that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sup _{z,w \in B} \big (\mathbb {E} |\Phi (z) \Phi (w)|^{p'}\big )^{1/p'} \le C'_{B,p}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}supz,w∈B(E|Φ(z)Φ(w)|′)1/p′≤CB,p′.In addition, since 1<pq<21<pq<2, by Lemma 4.1 and Proposition 2.4,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {E} \big [|F(z) F(w)|^{-pq}\big ] \le C_{pq} \max \big \{1,|z-w|^{2(1-pq)}\big \}, \qquad z,w \in \mathbb {C}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[|F(z)F(w)|-pq]≤Cpqmax{1,|z-w|2(1-pq)},z,w∈C.We also note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _{\epsilon }(F(z)) \lesssim \epsilon ^{q-2} |F(z)|^{-q}$$\end{document}χϵ(F(z))≲ϵq-2|F(z)|-q and further estimate\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\mathbb {E} \big [\big |\tilde{N}^\epsilon (B) - N^\epsilon (B) \big |^2\big ]\\&\quad \le C^2_B \cdot \epsilon ^2 \cdot \int _{B\times B} \mathbb {E} \big [ \chi _{\epsilon }(F(z)) \chi _{\epsilon }(F(w)) \Phi (z) \Phi (w)\big ] \, dA(z)dA(w) \\&\quad \lesssim C''_{B,p} \cdot \epsilon ^2 \cdot \int _{B\times B} \big (\mathbb {E}\big [ |\chi _{\epsilon }(F(z)) \chi _{\epsilon }(F(w))|^p\big ]\big )^{1/p} \, dA(z)dA(w) \\&\quad \lesssim C''_{B,p} \cdot \epsilon ^2 \cdot \epsilon ^{2q-4} \cdot \int _{B\times B} \big (\mathbb {E} \big [|F(z) F(w)|^{-pq}\big ]\big )^{1/p} \, dA(z)dA(w) \\&\quad \lesssim C''_{B,p,q} \cdot \epsilon ^{2q-2} \cdot \int _{B\times B} \max \{1,|z-w|^{2(1/p-q)}\} \, dA(z)dA(w). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[|N~ϵ(B)-Nϵ(B)|2]≤CB2·ϵ2·∫B×BE[χϵ(F(z))χϵ(F(w))Φ(z)Φ(w)]dA(z)dA(w)≲CB,p′′·ϵ2·∫B×B(E[|χϵ(F(z))χϵ(F(w))|p])1/pdA(z)dA(w)≲CB,p′′·ϵ2·ϵ2q-4·∫B×B(E[|F(z)F(w)|-pq])1/pdA(z)dA(w)≲CB,p,q′′·ϵ2q-2·∫B×Bmax{1,|z-w|2(1/p-q)}dA(z)dA(w).The previous integral is finite because 0<2(q-1/p)<2(2/p-1/p)=2/p <20<2(q-1/p)<2(2/p-1/p)=2/p<2, while the power of ε ϵ is positive, which proves (7.12).

Step 3. Finally, we combine (7.11) and (7.12) to conclude that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}N(B) < \infty $$\end{document}VarN(B)<∞ and obtain (7.10). □ □

Lemma 7.1 shows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N^\epsilon (B) \in L^2(\mathcal {F}_S,\textrm{d}\mathbb {P})$$\end{document}Nϵ(B)∈L2(FS,dP) and, therefore, by Proposition 7.2,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} N(B)\in L^2(\mathcal {F}_S,\textrm{d}\mathbb {P}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}N(B)∈L2(FS,dP).Hence, the number statistic N(B) can be expanded as7.13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} N(B) = \sum _{M,N \ge 0} Q_{M,N}(N(B)), \qquad Q_{M,N}(N(B)) \in C_{M,N}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}N(B)=∑M,N≥0QM,N(N(B)),QM,N(N(B))∈CM,N,into chaotic components (7.6) associated with the Gaussian space induced by S. We now calculate the chaos expansion of N(B) explicitly in the case of radial twisted kernels.

Theorem 7.3

Let F be a zero-mean GWHF satisfying the general assumptions and with a twisted kernel of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H(z)=P(|z|^2)$$\end{document}H(z)=P(|z|2) where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P:\mathbb {R} \rightarrow \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}P:R→C is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^6$$\end{document}C6. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B \subset \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}B⊂C be a bounded Borel set and consider the number statistic N(B). Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N(B) \in L^2(\mathcal {F}_S, \textrm{d}\mathbb {P})$$\end{document}N(B)∈L2(FS,dP), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_S$$\end{document}FS is the σ σ-algebra generated by S given by (7.4).

Consider the chaos decomposition associated with F and let (7.13) be corresponding expansion of N(B). Let N,M \ge 0N,M≥0. Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_{M,N}(N(B))=0$$\end{document}QM,N(N(B))=0 for N\not =MN≠M, while7.14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Q_{N,N}(N(B))&= \frac{1}{\pi }\sum _{k+l+j = N} c_{k,l} \int _B L_j\left( |F(z)|^2\right) L_k\left( \frac{|\mathcal {D}_1F(z)|^2}{1/2-\Delta H(0)}\right) \nonumber \\&\qquad \, L_{l}\left( \frac{|\mathcal {D}_2F(z)|^2}{-1/2-\Delta H(0)} \right) \, dA(z), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}QN,N(N(B))=1π∑k+l+j=Nck,l∫BLj|F(z)|2Lk|D1F(z)|21/2-ΔH(0)Ll|D2F(z)|2-1/2-ΔH(0)dA(z),where the integral is defined realization-wise and7.15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} c_{k,l}=\,&\frac{1}{\pi ^2}\int _{\mathbb {C}} \int _{\mathbb {C}} \bigl |(-\Delta H(0) + 1/2)|z|^2\nonumber \\ &- (-\Delta H(0) - 1/2)|w|^2\bigr | L_k(|z|^2)\, L_l(|w|^2) \,e^{-|z|^2-|w|^2} \,dA(z,w). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ck,l=1π2∫C∫C|(-ΔH(0)+1/2)|z|2-(-ΔH(0)-1/2)|w|2|Lk(|z|2)Ll(|w|2)e-|z|2-|w|2dA(z,w).

Proof

Step 1. We have already observed that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N(B) \in L^2(\mathcal {F}_S)$$\end{document}N(B)∈L2(FS). We thus arrive at the expansion7.17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\chi _\epsilon (\zeta _1) \big |(-\Delta H(0) + 1/2)|\zeta _2|^2- (-\Delta H(0) - 1/2)|\zeta _3|^2 \big | \nonumber \\&= \sum _{j,k,l\ge 0} a_{j, \epsilon } c_{k,l} L_j(|\zeta _1|^2) L_k(|\zeta _2|^2) L_l(|\zeta _3|^2), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}χϵ(ζ1)|(-ΔH(0)+1/2)|ζ2|2-(-ΔH(0)-1/2)|ζ3|2|=∑j,k,l≥0aj,ϵck,lLj(|ζ1|2)Lk(|ζ2|2)Ll(|ζ3|2),with convergence in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2\big (\mathbb {C}, \frac{1}{\pi ^3} e^{-|\zeta _1|^2-|\zeta _2|^2-|\zeta _3|^2} dA(\zeta _1) dA(\zeta _2) dA(\zeta _3)\big )$$\end{document}L2(C,1π3e-|ζ1|2-|ζ2|2-|ζ3|2dA(ζ1)dA(ζ2)dA(ζ3)).

Step 2. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z \in \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}z∈C be arbitrary but fixed. The covariance of the Gaussian vector\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (F(z), \mathcal {D}_1 F(z),\mathcal {D}_2 F(z)) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(F(z),D1F(z),D2F(z))is given by (2.5). Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H(z)=P(|z|^2)$$\end{document}H(z)=P(|z|2), it follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial H(0)=\bar{\partial } H(0)=\partial ^2 H(0) = \bar{\partial }^2 H(0)=0$$\end{document}∂H(0)=∂¯H(0)=∂2H(0)=∂¯2H(0)=0. Consequently,7.18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (\xi (z), \xi '(z), \xi ''(z)) := \bigg ( F(z), \frac{\mathcal {D}_1F(z)}{(-\Delta H(0) + 1/2)^{1/2}}, \frac{\mathcal {D}_2F(z)}{(-\Delta H(0) - 1/2)^{1/2}} \bigg ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(ξ(z),ξ′(z),ξ′′(z)):=(F(z),D1F(z)(-ΔH(0)+1/2)1/2,D2F(z)(-ΔH(0)-1/2)1/2)is a standard complex Gaussian vector and the map\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} L^2\big (\mathbb {C}, \tfrac{1}{\pi ^3} e^{-|\zeta _1|^2-|\zeta _2|^2-|\zeta _3|^2} dA(\zeta _1) dA(\zeta _2) dA(\zeta _3)\big )&\rightarrow L^2(\textrm{d}\mathbb {P}) \\ f&\mapsto f(\xi (z), \xi '(z), \xi ''(z)) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L2(C,1π3e-|ζ1|2-|ζ2|2-|ζ3|2dA(ζ1)dA(ζ2)dA(ζ3))→L2(dP)f↦f(ξ(z),ξ′(z),ξ′′(z))is an isometric embedding. Thus (7.17) translates into the almost sure equality7.19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\chi _\epsilon (F(z)) \big ||\mathcal {D}_1F(z)|^2- |\mathcal {D}_2F(z)|^2 \big |\nonumber \\&\qquad = \sum _{j,k,l\ge 0} a_{j, \epsilon } c_{k,l} L_j(|\xi (z)|^2) L_k(|\xi '(z)|^2) L_l(|\xi ''(z)|^2), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}χϵ(F(z))||D1F(z)|2-|D2F(z)|2|=∑j,k,l≥0aj,ϵck,lLj(|ξ(z)|2)Lk(|ξ′(z)|2)Ll(|ξ′′(z)|2),with convergence in quadratic mean for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z \in \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}z∈C.

We shall invoke Lemma 7.1 with the functions\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\phi _0(\zeta _1,\zeta _2,\zeta _3)=\chi _\epsilon (\zeta _1) \big | |\zeta _2|^2-|\zeta _3|^2 \big |, \\&\phi _{j,k,l}(\zeta _1,\zeta _2,\zeta _3)= L_j(|\zeta _1|^2) L_k(|\zeta _2|^2) L_l(|\zeta _3|^2), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϕ0(ζ1,ζ2,ζ3)=χϵ(ζ1)||ζ2|2-|ζ3|2|,ϕj,k,l(ζ1,ζ2,ζ3)=Lj(|ζ1|2)Lk(|ζ2|2)Ll(|ζ3|2),which satisfy the hypothesis of the lemma (with constants that depend on j, k, l). With the notation of Lemma 7.1,7.20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&Q_{M,N}\left[ \sum _{h=1}^{L_n} \chi _\epsilon (F(z_h)) \big ||\mathcal {D}_1F(z_h)|^2- |\mathcal {D}_2F(z_h)|^2 \big |\, \,\big |B \cap Q_h \big |\right] \nonumber \\&\quad =\sum _{h=1}^{L_n} \sum _{j,k,l\ge 0} a_{j, \epsilon } c_{k,l} Q_{M,N}\left[ L_j(|\xi (z_h) |^2) L_{k}(|\xi '(z_h) |^2) L_{l}(|\xi ''(z_h) |^2)\right] \,\big |B \cap Q_h \big | \end{aligned}$$\end{document}QM,N∑h=1Lnχϵ(F(zh))||D1F(zh)|2-|D2F(zh)|2||B∩Qh|=∑h=1Ln∑j,k,l≥0aj,ϵck,lQM,NLj(|ξ(zh)|2)Lk(|ξ′(zh)|2)Ll(|ξ′′(zh)|2)|B∩Qh|7.21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\quad ={\delta _{N=M}} \sum _{h=1}^{L_n} \sum _{j+k+l=N} a_{j, \epsilon } c_{k,l}L_j(|\xi (z_h) |^2) L_{k}(|\xi '(z_h) |^2) L_{l}(|\xi ''(z_h) |^2) \,\big |B \cap Q_h \big |. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}=δN=M∑h=1Ln∑j+k+l=Naj,ϵck,lLj(|ξ(zh)|2)Lk(|ξ′(zh)|2)Ll(|ξ′′(zh)|2)|B∩Qh|.By Lemma 7.1, as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}n→∞, the Riemann sums in (7.20) and (7.21) converge to the corresponding integrals in quadratic mean. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_{M,N}$$\end{document}QM,N is continuous in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(\textrm{d}\mathbb {P})$$\end{document}L2(dP), we conclude that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Q_{M,N}[N^\epsilon (B)]\! =\! \delta _{N=M} \!\!\int _B \sum _{j+k+l=N} a_{j, \epsilon } c_{k,l} L_j(|\xi (z)|^2) L_k(|\xi '(z)|^2) L_l(|\xi ''(z)|^2) \, dA(z). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}QM,N[Nϵ(B)]=δN=M∫B∑j+k+l=Naj,ϵck,lLj(|ξ(z)|2)Lk(|ξ′(z)|2)Ll(|ξ′′(z)|2)dA(z).By Proposition 7.2, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_{M,N}[N^\epsilon (B)]\longrightarrow Q_{M,N}[N(B)]$$\end{document}QM,N[Nϵ(B)]⟶QM,N[N(B)] in quadratic mean as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon \rightarrow 0^+$$\end{document}ϵ→0+, which, together with (7.16) gives (7.14) and the vanishing of the projection for N\not =MN≠M. □ □

Non-hyperuniformity of uncharged zeros: proof of theorem 1.2

In this section we prove Theorem 1.2, regarding the non-hyperuniformity of the uncharged zeros of a zero-mean GWHF F with the specific twisted kernel8.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H(z) = (1-|z|^2) e^{-\frac{1}{2} |z|^2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}H(z)=(1-|z|2)e-12|z|2.We analyze the zero set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{F=0\}$$\end{document}{F=0} on the observation disk \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B=B_R(0)$$\end{document}B=BR(0) centered at 0.

Lower bound

Let us consider the chaos decomposition of the number statistic N(B). The gist of the proof is to estimate the second order chaotic component \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_{2,2}(N(B))$$\end{document}Q2,2(N(B)). By the orthogonality of the chaos decomposition (as in (7.7)), we then have the estimate\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[N(B)] \ge {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[Q_{2,2}(N(B))]. $$\end{document}Var[N(B)]≥Var[Q2,2(N(B))].We will show that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[Q_{2,2}(N(B))] \gtrsim R^2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Var[Q2,2(N(B))]≳R2.(On the other hand, one can check that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[Q_{1,1}(N(B))] = O(R)$$\end{document}Var[Q1,1(N(B))]=O(R), which is not enough for our purpose.)

We shall inspect the explicit expression for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_{2,2}(N(B))$$\end{document}Q2,2(N(B)) given in Theorem 7.3. In our case,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Delta H(0) = \partial \bar{\partial } H(0) = -\frac{3}{2}. $$\end{document}ΔH(0)=∂∂¯H(0)=-32.We follow the notation from Sect. 7.3 and specialize (7.18) to8.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (\xi (z), \xi '(z), \xi ''(z)) = \Big ( F(z), \tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}} \mathcal {D}_1F(z), \mathcal {D}_2F(z) \Big ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(ξ(z),ξ′(z),ξ′′(z))=(F(z),12D1F(z),D2F(z)),whose entries are independent and distributed as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {N}_\mathbb {C}(0,1)$$\end{document}NC(0,1), for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z\in \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}z∈C.
