The following proposition is an application of the so-called Feynman-diagram method and enables us to compute \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {E}[\phi (z)\phi (w)]$$\end{document}E[ϕ(z)ϕ(w)] term-by-term according to (8.4).

Proposition 8.1

Suppose that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\alpha ,\beta ,\gamma ,\delta )$$\end{document}(α,β,γ,δ) is a complex Gaussian random vector, where each coordinate has a standard complex Gaussian distribution, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {E}(\alpha \overline{\beta })=0$$\end{document}E(αβ¯)=0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {E}(\gamma \overline{\delta })=0$$\end{document}E(γδ¯)=0. Then: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {E}[L_1(|\alpha |^2) L_1(|\beta |^2) L_1(|\gamma |^2) L_1(|\delta |^2)] = \Big |\mathbb {E}(\alpha \overline{\gamma })\mathbb {E}(\beta \overline{\delta }) + \mathbb {E}(\alpha \overline{\delta }) \mathbb {E}(\beta \overline{\gamma } )\Big |^2 $$\end{document}E[L1(|α|2)L1(|β|2)L1(|γ|2)L1(|δ|2)]=|E(αγ¯)E(βδ¯)+E(αδ¯)E(βγ¯)|2,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {E}[L_1(|\alpha |^2) L_1(|\beta |^2) L_2(|\gamma |^2) ] = 2 \left| \mathbb {E}(\alpha \overline{\gamma }) \mathbb {E}(\beta \overline{\gamma }) \right| ^2 $$\end{document}E[L1(|α|2)L1(|β|2)L2(|γ|2)]=2E(αγ¯)E(βγ¯)2,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {E}[L_2(|\alpha |^2) L_2(|\gamma |^2 ) ] = \left| \mathbb {E}(\alpha \overline{\gamma })\right| ^4$$\end{document}E[L2(|α|2)L2(|γ|2)]=E(αγ¯)4.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {E}[L_1(|\alpha |^2) L_1(|\beta |^2) L_1(|\gamma |^2) L_1(|\delta |^2)] = \Big |\mathbb {E}(\alpha \overline{\gamma })\mathbb {E}(\beta \overline{\delta }) + \mathbb {E}(\alpha \overline{\delta }) \mathbb {E}(\beta \overline{\gamma } )\Big |^2 $$\end{document}E[L1(|α|2)L1(|β|2)L1(|γ|2)L1(|δ|2)]=|E(αγ¯)E(βδ¯)+E(αδ¯)E(βγ¯)|2,

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {E}[L_1(|\alpha |^2) L_1(|\beta |^2) L_1(|\gamma |^2) L_1(|\delta |^2)] = \Big |\mathbb {E}(\alpha \overline{\gamma })\mathbb {E}(\beta \overline{\delta }) + \mathbb {E}(\alpha \overline{\delta }) \mathbb {E}(\beta \overline{\gamma } )\Big |^2 $$\end{document}E[L1(|α|2)L1(|β|2)L1(|γ|2)L1(|δ|2)]=|E(αγ¯)E(βδ¯)+E(αδ¯)E(βγ¯)|2,

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {E}[L_1(|\alpha |^2) L_1(|\beta |^2) L_2(|\gamma |^2) ] = 2 \left| \mathbb {E}(\alpha \overline{\gamma }) \mathbb {E}(\beta \overline{\gamma }) \right| ^2 $$\end{document}E[L1(|α|2)L1(|β|2)L2(|γ|2)]=2E(αγ¯)E(βγ¯)2,

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {E}[L_1(|\alpha |^2) L_1(|\beta |^2) L_2(|\gamma |^2) ] = 2 \left| \mathbb {E}(\alpha \overline{\gamma }) \mathbb {E}(\beta \overline{\gamma }) \right| ^2 $$\end{document}E[L1(|α|2)L1(|β|2)L2(|γ|2)]=2E(αγ¯)E(βγ¯)2,

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {E}[L_2(|\alpha |^2) L_2(|\gamma |^2 ) ] = \left| \mathbb {E}(\alpha \overline{\gamma })\right| ^4$$\end{document}E[L2(|α|2)L2(|γ|2)]=E(αγ¯)4.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {E}[L_2(|\alpha |^2) L_2(|\gamma |^2 ) ] = \left| \mathbb {E}(\alpha \overline{\gamma })\right| ^4$$\end{document}E[L2(|α|2)L2(|γ|2)]=E(αγ¯)4.

The proof of this proposition is provided in Sect. 8.3 below. Here we apply it to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha , \beta \in \{\xi (z),\xi '(z),\xi ''(z)\}$$\end{document}α,β∈{ξ(z),ξ′(z),ξ′′(z)} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma , \delta \in \{\xi (w),\xi '(w),\xi ''(w)\}$$\end{document}γ,δ∈{ξ(w),ξ′(w),ξ′′(w)} such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \ne \beta $$\end{document}α≠β, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \ne \delta $$\end{document}γ≠δ. This allows us to continue from (8.6), using the form of ϕ ϕ given in (8.4). The expectation of each term is then expressed (via Proposition 8.1) in terms of covariances between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\xi (z),\xi '(z),\xi ''(z))$$\end{document}(ξ(z),ξ′(z),ξ′′(z)) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\xi (w),\xi '(w),\xi ''(w))$$\end{document}(ξ(w),ξ′(w),ξ′′(w)). These covariance computations are explicitly carried out using the relations in Lemma 2.1, and recalling the definitions in (1.3), (1.6), (8.1), and (8.2). We include the details in Appendix A. Using (8.5) and combining all the resulting expressions, we obtain that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathbb {E}[\phi (z)\phi (w)]= g(|z-w|^2), \text { where } $$\end{document}E[ϕ(z)ϕ(w)]=g(|z-w|2),where\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} g(s)= & \frac{2}{729} e^{-2s} (2091 - 22110 s + 62628 s^2 - 77836 s^3 \\ & + 48325 s^4 - 15040 s^5 + 2156 s^6 - 116 s^7 + 2 s^8), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}g(s)=2729e-2s(2091-22110s+62628s2-77836s3+48325s4-15040s5+2156s6-116s7+2s8),see the accompanying notebook for symbolic calculations [19]. This leads to the explicit bound8.7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}N(B)\ge & {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}Q_{2,2}(N(B)) = \frac{1}{\pi ^2}\int _B\int _B g(|z-w|^2) dA(z) dA(w)\nonumber \\= & \frac{1}{\pi ^2}\int _{B} \big (g(|\cdot |^2) * 1_{B}\big )(z)\,dA(z), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}VarN(B)≥VarQ2,2(N(B))=1π2∫B∫Bg(|z-w|2)dA(z)dA(w)=1π2∫B(g(|·|2)∗1B)(z)dA(z),which we now inspect. One can easily verify that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \int _\mathbb {C}g\big (|z|^2\big ) \,dA(z) = \pi \int _0^\infty g(s) ds= \frac{7}{81}\pi , $$\end{document}∫Cg(|z|2)dA(z)=π∫0∞g(s)ds=781π,see for example the accompanying notebook [19]. For us the important fact is that this number is not equal to zero. We also note that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \int _\mathbb {C}|z| |g(|z|^2)| dA(z) <\infty . $$\end{document}∫C|z||g(|z|2)|dA(z)<∞.We shall need the following standard estimate, which can be found in [30, Lemma 8.3].

Lemma 8.2

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_R=B_R(0)$$\end{document}BR=BR(0). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h:\mathbb {C}\rightarrow \mathbb {R} $$\end{document}h:C→R be integrable with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int _{\mathbb {C}} h(z)dA(z) \ne 0$$\end{document}∫Ch(z)dA(z)≠0 and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ C_h:=\int _\mathbb {C}|z| |h(z)| dA(z) < \infty . $$\end{document}Ch:=∫C|z||h(z)|dA(z)<∞.There exists a universal constant C such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \bigg | \int _{B_R} h * 1_{B_R} - \bigg ( \int _\mathbb {C}h(z)dA(z) \bigg ) |B_R| \bigg | \le CC_hR, \quad R>0. $$\end{document}|∫BRh∗1BR-(∫Ch(z)dA(z))|BR||≤CChR,R>0.

Applying Lemma 8.2, we may continue from (8.7) to conclude that8.8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}N(B)&\ge \frac{1}{\pi ^2}\int _{B_R} \big (g(|\cdot |^2) * 1_{B_R}\big )(z)\,dA(z) \nonumber \\&= \frac{1}{\pi ^2}\int _{\mathbb {C}} g\big (|z|^2\big ) \,dA(z) \cdot \pi R^2 + O(R) = \frac{7}{81} R^2 + O(R) \ge c R^2 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}VarN(B)≥1π2∫BR(g(|·|2)∗1BR)(z)dA(z)=1π2∫Cg(|z|2)dA(z)·πR2+O(R)=781R2+O(R)≥cR2for large enough R and a strictly positive constant c. To conclude the proof of the lower bound, we need to observe that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}N(B_R) >0$$\end{document}VarN(BR)>0 for any R>0R>0. This is the case, in general, for any Euclidean stationary point process on the plane with positive and finite first intensity (see, e.g., Lemma 10.1 below). In our case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{F=0\}$$\end{document}{F=0} is stationary because F has zero mean (and is invariant under twisted shifts), while the first intensity of the zero set is finite and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ge \tfrac{1}{\pi }$$\end{document}≥1π, as shown in [30, Theorem 1.6].

Upper bound

We now look into an upper bound for the variance of the number statistic.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z \in \mathbb {C}\setminus \{0\}$$\end{document}z∈C\{0}, consider the Gaussian vector8.9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} X(z) = (\mathcal {D}_1 F(z), \mathcal {D}_2 F(z), \mathcal {D}_1 F(0), \mathcal {D}_2 F(0), F(z), F(0)), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}X(z)=(D1F(z),D2F(z),D1F(0),D2F(0),F(z),F(0)),and write its covariance as8.10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Gamma (z) = \begin{bmatrix} A & B \\ B^* & C \end{bmatrix} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Γ(z)=ABB∗Cwith \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A \in \mathbb {C}^{4\times 4}$$\end{document}A∈C4×4. Let Z be a standard complex Gaussian vector in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {C}^{4}$$\end{document}C4 (so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Cov}\,}}(Z)=I \in \mathbb {C}^{4 \times 4}$$\end{document}Cov(Z)=I∈C4×4). The two point function (6.9) can be expressed by Gaussian regression as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tau (z,0,0) = (1-|H(z)|^2)^{-1}\cdot \mathbb {E} \big [ f((A-BC^{-1}B^*)^{1/2} Z) \big ], \quad z\not = 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}τ(z,0,0)=(1-|H(z)|2)-1·E[f((A-BC-1B∗)1/2Z)],z≠0,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(u_1, \dots , u_4)=\tfrac{1}{\pi ^2}\big ||u_1|^2-|u_2|^2 \big | \cdot \big ||u_3|^2-|u_4|^2 \big |$$\end{document}f(u1,⋯,u4)=1π2||u1|2-|u2|2|·||u3|2-|u4|2|. We note that8.11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |f(u) - f(u')| \lesssim |u-u'| \cdot (1+|u|^s+|u'|^s), \qquad u,u' \in \mathbb {C}^4, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|f(u)-f(u′)|≲|u-u′|·(1+|u|s+|u′|s),u,u′∈C4,and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|f(u)| \lesssim (1+|u|^s)$$\end{document}|f(u)|≲(1+|u|s) for some s>0s>0.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\Gamma }$$\end{document}Γ~ be obtained from Γ Γ by replacing with 0 every entry that corresponds to correlations between a function of z and a function evaluated at 0. (Actually, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{C}=I$$\end{document}C~=I.)

By the fast decay of H and its derivatives,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \Gamma - \tilde{\Gamma }\Vert \lesssim e^{-2c|z|^2}, \qquad \Vert \Gamma \Vert , \Vert \tilde{\Gamma }\Vert \lesssim 1, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Γ-Γ~‖≲e-2c|z|2,‖Γ‖,‖Γ~‖≲1,for some constant c>0c>0. Proof of Proposition 8.1

The proof relies on the well-known diagram method (see Janson [37]), which has been previously used in somewhat similar situations, e.g., in [13, 57, 12]. We briefly recall this method.

We further note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {E}(\alpha \gamma ) =0$$\end{document}E(αγ)=0 for any circularly symmetric complex Gaussian random vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\alpha ,\gamma )$$\end{document}(α,γ). Since the degree of each vertex must be 1, there must be an edge joining α α with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\gamma }$$\end{document}γ¯ or with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\delta }$$\end{document}δ¯; the remaining vertex must be joined to β β. Similarly, there must be an edge joining γ γ with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\alpha }$$\end{document}α¯ or with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\beta }$$\end{document}β¯; the remaining vertex will be joined with δ δ. These choices are independent. Therefore, the sum of values of the resulting four diagrams is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\left( \mathbb {E}(\alpha \overline{\gamma })\mathbb {E}(\beta \overline{\delta }) + \mathbb {E}(\alpha \overline{\delta }) \mathbb {E}(\beta \overline{\gamma } ) \right) \left( \mathbb {E}(\gamma \overline{\alpha }) \mathbb {E}(\delta \overline{\beta }) + \mathbb {E}(\gamma \overline{\beta }) \mathbb {E}(\delta \overline{\alpha }) \right) \\&\qquad = \Big |\mathbb {E}(\alpha \overline{\gamma })\mathbb {E}(\beta \overline{\delta }) + \mathbb {E}(\alpha \overline{\delta }) \mathbb {E}(\beta \overline{\gamma } )\Big |^2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E(αγ¯)E(βδ¯)+E(αδ¯)E(βγ¯)E(γα¯)E(δβ¯)+E(γβ¯)E(δα¯)=|E(αγ¯)E(βδ¯)+E(αδ¯)E(βγ¯)|2.Plugging this into (8.14), we obtain the first item.

For the second part, we need to compute\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathbb {E}[L_1(|\alpha |^2) L_1(|\beta |^2) L_2(|\gamma |^2) ]. $$\end{document}E[L1(|α|2)L1(|β|2)L2(|γ|2)].The corresponding diagrams are bi-partite graphs, with edges between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_1 = \{\alpha ,\overline{\alpha }, \beta , \overline{\beta }\}$$\end{document}V1={α,α¯,β,β¯} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_2 = \{\gamma , \overline{\gamma }, \gamma , \overline{\gamma }\}$$\end{document}V2={γ,γ¯,γ,γ¯} (that is, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_2$$\end{document}V2 is a set of four vertices carrying two labels of γ γ and two labels of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\gamma }$$\end{document}γ¯). By the same arguments as before, α α must be joined to one of two copies of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\gamma }$$\end{document}γ¯ (and the remaining copy must be joined to β β), and the first copy of γ γ must be joined to either \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\alpha }$$\end{document}α¯ or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\beta }$$\end{document}β¯ (and the remaining vertex must be joined to the second copy of γ γ). These four diagrams have the value\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathbb {E}(\alpha \overline{\gamma })\mathbb {E}(\beta \overline{\gamma })\mathbb {E}(\overline{\alpha }\gamma ) \mathbb {E}(\overline{\beta }\gamma ) = \left| \mathbb {E}(\alpha \overline{\gamma }) \mathbb {E}(\beta \overline{\gamma }) \right| ^2. $$\end{document}E(αγ¯)E(βγ¯)E(α¯γ)E(β¯γ)=E(αγ¯)E(βγ¯)2.Considering the normalizing factor in (8.14), we establish the second item.

For the computation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {E}[ L_2(|\alpha |^2) L_2(|\gamma |^2)]$$\end{document}E[L2(|α|2)L2(|γ|2)] in the third part, the corresponding diagrams are bi-partite graphs with edges between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_1 = \{ \alpha ,\overline{\alpha },\alpha ,\overline{\alpha }\}$$\end{document}V1={α,α¯,α,α¯} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_2 = \{\gamma , \overline{\gamma }, \gamma , \overline{\gamma }\}$$\end{document}V2={γ,γ¯,γ,γ¯}. Again there are four diagrams, resulting from a choice of edge joining the first copy of α α to one of the two copies of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\gamma }$$\end{document}γ¯, and an edge joining the first copy of γ γ to one of the two copies of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\alpha }$$\end{document}α¯. The value of each such diagram is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\mathbb {E}(\alpha \overline{\gamma })|^4$$\end{document}|E(αγ¯)|4. The normalizing factor in (8.14) is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tfrac{1}{2! 2!}$$\end{document}12!2!, which yields the result.

Applications

Gaussian entire functions

We start by identifying Gaussian entire functions and their iterated covariant derivatives as GWHF.

Lemma 9.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} Proof

When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek}
