We can now prove the hyperuniformity of the zeros of a GEF with non-trivial mean.

Proof of Theorem 1.3

By Lemma 9.1, we identify \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G=G_0+G_1$$\end{document}G=G0+G1 with a GWHF with twisted kernel\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H(z)=e^{-\tfrac{1}{2}|{z}|^2} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}H(z)=e-12|z|2by setting\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F_j(z) = e^{-\tfrac{1}{2}|{z}|^2} G_j(z), \qquad j=0,1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Fj(z)=e-12|z|2Gj(z),j=0,1.By Lemma 9.1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F:=F_0+F_1$$\end{document}F:=F0+F1 satisfies the assumptions of Theorem 1.1, so it suffices to observe that all its charges are equal to 1. This follows from the analyticity of G. Indeed, if F(z)=0F(z)=0 then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\,\textrm{Jac}\,}}F(z) = |\partial F(z)|^2 - |\bar{\partial } F(z)|^2 \ge 0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}JacF(z)=|∂F(z)|2-|∂¯F(z)|2≥0because\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \bar{\partial } F(z) = \Big (\bar{\partial } G(z) -\frac{z}{2} G(z)\Big ) e^{-\tfrac{1}{2}|{z}|^2} = 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂¯F(z)=(∂¯G(z)-z2G(z))e-12|z|2=0,while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Jac}\,}}F(z) \not = 0$$\end{document}JacF(z)≠0 by Lemma 3.1. Therefore \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa _z = {{\,\textrm{sgn}\,}}{{\,\textrm{Jac}\,}}F(z) =1$$\end{document}κz=sgnJacF(z)=1, as claimed. □ □

We now look into the critical points of translation-invariant GEF.

Proof of Theorems 1.4 and 1.5

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G=G_0$$\end{document}G=G0 be the zero-mean Gaussian entire function given by (1.4). By Lemma 9.1,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F(z)= e^{-\tfrac{1}{2}|{z}|^2}\, \bar{\partial }^* G(z) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F(z)=e-12|z|2∂¯∗G(z)is a GWHF with twisted kernel \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H(z) = (1-|z|^2)e^{-|{z}|^2/2}$$\end{document}H(z)=(1-|z|2)e-|z|2/2. Hence Theorem 1.4 follows immediately from Theorem 1.2.

Second, because multiplication by a smooth non-vanishing factor does not alter indices (see e.g., [30, Lemma 6.5]) the charge of F at a zero is exactly the index of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\partial }^* G$$\end{document}∂¯∗G. Thus, Theorem 1.1 (or [30, Theorem 1.12]) implies that9.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[\mathcal {N}_R^+ - \mathcal {N}_R^-] \le C R, \qquad R>1, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Var[NR+-NR-]≤CR,R>1,for a constant C>0C>0. On the other hand \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {N}_R^+ + \mathcal {N}_R^-$$\end{document}NR++NR- is the total number of zeros of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\partial }^* G$$\end{document}∂¯∗G in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_R(0)$$\end{document}BR(0) and therefore Theorem 1.2 gives9.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} k R^2 \le {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[\mathcal {N}_R^+ + \mathcal {N}_R^-]\le K R^2, \qquad R>1, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}kR2≤Var[NR++NR-]≤KR2,R>1,for adequate constants k,K>0k,K>0. As a consequence, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[\mathcal {N}_R^+] \lesssim {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[\mathcal {N}_R^+ + \mathcal {N}_R^-] + {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[\mathcal {N}_R^+ - \mathcal {N}_R^-] \lesssim (K+C) R^2, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Var[NR+]≲Var[NR++NR-]+Var[NR+-NR-]≲(K+C)R2,while\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} k R^2&\le {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[\mathcal {N}_R^+ + \mathcal {N}_R^-] = {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[2\mathcal {N}_R^+ - (\mathcal {N}_R^+ - \mathcal {N}_R^-)] \\&\le 8 {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[\mathcal {N}_R^+] + 2 {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[\mathcal {N}_R^+ - \mathcal {N}_R^-] \le 8 {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[\mathcal {N}_R^+] + 2 C R. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}kR2≤Var[NR++NR-]=Var[2NR+-(NR+-NR-)]≤8Var[NR+]+2Var[NR+-NR-]≤8Var[NR+]+2CR.A similar argument applies to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {N}_R^-$$\end{document}NR- and proves (1.21) for large R. To conclude the proof we observe that the each of processes defining \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {N}_R^\pm $$\end{document}NR± is stationary and has finite first intensities — c.f. Remark 2.5 or [30, Proof of Lemma 3.3] — so that Lemma 10.1 below implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}[\mathcal {N}_R^\pm ]$$\end{document}Var[NR±] cannot vanish for any R>0R>0. Hence, (1.21) also holds in the range R \ge 1R≥1. □ □

Weighted entire functions

Theorems 1.4 and 1.5 can also be formulated in terms of the weighted magnitude9.7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} A(z) = e^{-\tfrac{1}{2}|{z}|^2} |G_0(z)| \end{aligned}$$\end{document}A(z)=e-12|z|2|G0(z)|associated with the translation-invariant Gaussian entire function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_0$$\end{document}G0 given by (1.4). Near points where the amplitude A does not vanish, it is smooth and its gradient is related to the covariant derivative of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_0$$\end{document}G0 by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |\nabla A(z)| = e^{-\tfrac{1}{2}|{z}|^2} |\bar{\partial }^* G_0(z)| = |F(z)|, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|∇A(z)|=e-12|z|2|∂¯∗G0(z)|=|F(z)|,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F(z) = e^{-\tfrac{1}{2}|{z}|^2} \bar{\partial }^* G_0(z)$$\end{document}F(z)=e-12|z|2∂¯∗G0(z). Hence each critical point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_0$$\end{document}z0 of A (in the usual real sense) that is not a zero of A is a zero of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\partial }^* G_0(z)$$\end{document}∂¯∗G0(z). Conversely, a zero of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\partial }^* G_0$$\end{document}∂¯∗G0 is almost surely not a zero of A, as this would correspond to a degenerate zero of the GWHF \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e^{-|z|^2/2} G_0(z)$$\end{document}e-|z|2/2G0(z), contradicting Lemma 3.1.

Thus the critical points of A are either zeros of A or critical points of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_0$$\end{document}G0, as discussed Sect. 1.5. Let us further inspect a critical point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_0$$\end{document}z0 of A that is not a zero. Near \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_0$$\end{document}z0 we can write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G(z)=L(z)^2$$\end{document}G(z)=L(z)2 with L analytic and compute\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 2 \partial A = A^{(1,0)} - i A^{(0,1)} = -{\frac{\overline{L}}{L}} \cdot F. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}2∂A=A(1,0)-iA(0,1)=-L¯L·F.The factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\overline{L}}/{L}}$$\end{document}L¯/L is smooth (in the real sense) and non-zero near \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_0$$\end{document}z0 and therefore does not affect the corresponding charge (see e.g., [30, Lemma 6.5]). Thus, the charge of F at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_0$$\end{document}z0 is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \kappa _z = {{\,\textrm{sgn}\,}}\Big [ \big [A^{(1,1)}\big ]^2 - A^{(2,0)} A^{(0,2)} \Big ], \end{aligned}$$\end{document}κz=sgn[[A(1,1)]2-A(2,0)A(0,2)],that is, the opposite of the sign of the determinant of the Hessian matrix of A at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_0$$\end{document}z0. As a consequence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa _z=1$$\end{document}κz=1 if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_0$$\end{document}z0 is a saddle point of A, while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa _z=-1$$\end{document}κz=-1 if A has a local maximum or local minimum at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_0$$\end{document}z0. In addition, by an argument based on superharmonicity one can see that local minima of A are not possible (as these correspond to zeros of A) [34, Section 8.2.2]) [17, Lemma 3.1].

Hence,9.8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {N}_R^+ = \# \text{ saddle } \text{ points } \text{ of } A \text{ in } B_R(0), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}NR+=#saddlepointsofAinBR(0),9.9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {N}_R^- = \# \text{ local } \text{ maxima } \text{ of } A \text{ in } B_R(0). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}NR-=#localmaximaofAinBR(0).The second order statistics of local extrema and saddle points of A thus satisfy (9.5), (9.6), and (1.21).

Time-frequency analysis

Given a non-zero Schwartz function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g \in \mathcal {S}(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}g∈S(R), the short-time Fourier transform (STFT) of a distribution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\in \mathcal {S}'(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}f∈S′(R) is defined by (1.27), where the integral is interpreted in the distributional sense (action on a test function). The modulation space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M^\infty (\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}M∞(R) consists of all tempered distributions with bounded STFT:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} M^\infty (\mathbb {R}) = \{f \in \mathcal {S}'(\mathbb {R}): V_g f \in L^\infty (\mathbb {R}^2)\}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}M∞(R)={f∈S′(R):Vgf∈L∞(R2)}.Historically, this space was first considered with respect to the Gaussian window function (in which case the STFT can be identified with the Bargmann transform [8]). In fact, it is easy to show that different choices of (non-zero, Schwartz) window functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_1, g_2 \in \mathcal {S}(\mathbb {R}) \setminus \{0\}$$\end{document}g1,g2∈S(R)\{0} define the same space, and that moreover there exists a constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{g_1,g_2}>0$$\end{document}Cg1,g2>0 such that9.10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert V_{g_1} f \Vert _\infty \le C_{g_1,g_2} \Vert V_{g_2} f \Vert _\infty , \qquad f \in M^\infty (\mathbb {R}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Vg1f‖∞≤Cg1,g2‖Vg2f‖∞,f∈M∞(R),see, e.g., [9] for the modern theory of modulation spaces.

The space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M^\infty (\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}M∞(R) contains \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}L2(R) and also all distributions commonly used in signal processing, such as the Dirac measure, sums of Dirac measures located along well-spread sets of points, and also their Fourier transforms [9].

We are mainly interested in a so-called signal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \in M^\infty (\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}f∈M∞(R) impacted by additive noise. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}$$\end{document}W be standard complex white noise on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}R, that is, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} \frac{d}{dt} \big (W_1 + i W_2\big )$$\end{document}W=12ddt(W1+iW2), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_1$$\end{document}W1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_2$$\end{document}W2 are independent copies of the Wiener process (Brownian motion with almost surely continuous paths), and the derivative is taken in the distributional sense. Then, almost every realization of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}$$\end{document}W is a tempered distribution and we can consider \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_g \mathcal {W}$$\end{document}VgW. For more details, see [30, Section 6.1], or [7] for a different approach.

The following Lemma identifies the STFT of a distribution impacted by additive complex white noise with a GWHF.

Lemma 9.2

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g \in \mathcal {S}(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}g∈S(R) be normalized by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert g\Vert _2=1$$\end{document}‖g‖2=1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \in M^\infty (\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}f∈M∞(R), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}$$\end{document}W standard complex white noise. Set9.11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F(z) := e^{-i xy} \cdot V_g \, (f+\mathcal {W}) \big (\bar{z}/\sqrt{\pi }\big ), \qquad z=x+iy. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F(z):=e-ixy·Vg(f+W)(z¯/π),z=x+iy.Then F is a GWHF with twisted kernel\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H(z) = e^{-i xy} \cdot V_g g \big (\bar{z}/\sqrt{\pi } \big ), \qquad z=x+iy. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}H(z)=e-ixy·Vgg(z¯/π),z=x+iy.In addition, conditions (1.8), (1.9), (1.11), (1.12), (1.14), and (1.15) are satisfied.

Proof

Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g \in \mathcal {S}(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}g∈S(R), it is easy to see that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H \in \mathcal {S}(\mathbb {R}^2)$$\end{document}H∈S(R2) — see, e.g., [25, Proposition 1.42] — which proves (1.15). In the zero mean case, the rest of the lemma is contained in [30, Lemma 6.1], so we focus on the mean function\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F_1(z) = F_{1,g}(z) := e^{-i xy} \cdot V_g \, f \big (\bar{z}/\sqrt{\pi }\big ), \qquad z=x+iy, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F1(z)=F1,g(z):=e-ixy·Vgf(z¯/π),z=x+iy,where, for convenience, we stress the dependence on the window function g. The assumption \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \in M^\infty (\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}f∈M∞(R) means that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F \in L^\infty (\mathbb {R}^2)$$\end{document}F∈L∞(R2), so it remains to inspect the twisted derivatives.

Taking the distributional interpretation of the formula defining the STFT (1.27) into account, a direct calculation gives\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {D}_1 F_{1,g}(z)&= \sqrt{\pi } F_{1, \mathcal {P} g}(z) -\tfrac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi }} F_{1, g'} (z), \\ \mathcal {D}_2 F_{1,g}(z)&= -\sqrt{\pi } F_{1, \mathcal {P} g}(z) -\tfrac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi }} F_{1, g'} (z), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}D1F1,g(z)=πF1,Pg(z)-12πF1,g′(z),D2F1,g(z)=-πF1,Pg(z)-12πF1,g′(z),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}g(t)=tg(t)$$\end{document}Pg(t)=tg(t). Since g'g′ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}g$$\end{document}Pg are non-zero Schwartz functions, the norm equivalence (9.10) implies that, for j=1,2j=1,2,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \mathcal {D}_j F_{1,g}\Vert _{\infty } \lesssim \Vert F_{1, \mathcal {P}g}\Vert _{\infty } + \Vert F_{1, g'}\Vert _{\infty } = \Vert V_{\mathcal {P}g} f \Vert _{\infty } + \Vert V_{g'} f \Vert _{\infty } \lesssim \Vert V_g f\Vert _\infty < \infty , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖DjF1,g‖∞≲‖F1,Pg‖∞+‖F1,g′‖∞=‖VPgf‖∞+‖Vg′f‖∞≲‖Vgf‖∞<∞,because \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \in M^\infty (\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}f∈M∞(R). □ □

We can now justify the application of our results to time-frequency analysis.

Proof of Theorem 1.6

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g(t)=2^{1/4}e^{-\pi t^2}$$\end{document}g(t)=21/4e-πt2, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}t∈R. By Lemma 9.2,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F(z) := e^{-i xy} \,V_g (f+\mathcal {W})\big (\bar{z}/\sqrt{\pi }\big ) = 2^{1/4} \,e^{-i xy} \,V (f+\mathcal {W}) \big (\bar{z}/\sqrt{\pi }\big ), \qquad z=x+iy \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F(z):=e-ixyVg(f+W)(z¯/π)=21/4e-ixyV(f+W)(z¯/π),z=x+iyis a GWHF and the assumptions of Theorem 1.1 are satisfied. In addition, the twisted kernel is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H(z)=e^{-|z|^2/2}$$\end{document}H(z)=e-|z|2/2 and F can be further related to a Gaussian entire function G by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F(z) = e^{-|z|^2/2} G(z)$$\end{document}F(z)=e-|z|2/2G(z). As in the proof of Theorem 1.3, all the charges of F are 1. The zeros of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S(f+\mathcal {W})=|V(f+\mathcal {W})|^2$$\end{document}S(f+W)=|V(f+W)|2 are related to those of F by the map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z \mapsto \bar{z}/\sqrt{\pi }$$\end{document}z↦z¯/π, and Theorem 1.1 (or Theorem 1.3) implies (1.25).

Secondly, in the zero mean case f=0f=0, the (Gaussian) spectrogram of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}$$\end{document}W is related to the weighted amplitude of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G=G_0$$\end{document}G=G0 by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S\,\mathcal {W}(x/\sqrt{\pi },-\xi /\sqrt{\pi }) = \tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,(A(x+i\xi ))^2$$\end{document}SW(x/π,-ξ/π)=12(A(x+iξ))2, c.f. (1.24) and (9.7). Thus, the map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z=x+i\xi \mapsto \bar{z}/\sqrt{\pi }$$\end{document}z=x+iξ↦z¯/π relates the set of local maxima of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S\,\mathcal {W}$$\end{document}SW to that of A, denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {N}_R^-$$\end{document}NR- in (9.9), which in turn satisfies (1.21). This gives (1.26). □ □

Proof of Theorem 1.7

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g:= \Vert h_1\Vert _2^{-1} h_1$$\end{document}g:=‖h1‖2-1h1. By Lemma 9.2,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F(z) := e^{-i xy} \cdot V_{g} \, \mathcal {W} \big (\bar{z}/\sqrt{\pi }\big ), \qquad z=x+iy, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F(z):=e-ixy·VgW(z¯/π),z=x+iy,defines a zero-mean GWHF function with twisted kernel\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H(z)=e^{-ixy} V_{g}g(\bar{z}/\sqrt{\pi })= (1-|z|^2)e^{-\tfrac{1}{2}|{z}|^2}, \qquad z=x+iy, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}H(z)=e-ixyVgg(z¯/π)=(1-|z|2)e-12|z|2,z=x+iy,as can be verified with a direct calculation (the last equality is a special case of the so-called Hermite-Laguerre connection [25, Theorem (1.104)]; see also [30, Section 6.4]). By Lemma 9.2, the hypothesis of Theorem 1.2 is satisfied, and the desired conclusion follows. □ □

Proof of Theorem 1.8

Without loss of generality we may assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert g\Vert _2=1$$\end{document}‖g‖2=1, and invoke Lemma 9.2. If z=a+ibz=a+ib is a zero of the GWHF (9.11), then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _x F(z)&= \frac{e^{-i ab}}{\sqrt{\pi }} \partial _x \big (V_g \, (f+\mathcal {W})\big ) (a/\sqrt{\pi },-b/\sqrt{\pi }), \\ \partial _y F(z)&= -\frac{e^{-i ab}}{\sqrt{\pi }} \partial _y\big (V_g \, (f+\mathcal {W})\big )(a/\sqrt{\pi },-b/\sqrt{\pi }), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂xF(z)=e-iabπ∂x(Vg(f+W))(a/π,-b/π),∂yF(z)=-e-iabπ∂y(Vg(f+W))(a/π,-b/π),and, consequently, the corresponding charge is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} -{{\,\textrm{sgn}\,}}\Im [\partial _x F(z) \cdot \overline{\partial _y F(z)}] = \mu _{\bar{z}/\sqrt{\pi }} (f+\mathcal {W}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}-sgnℑ[∂xF(z)·∂yF(z)¯]=μz¯/π(f+W).Thus the desired conclusion follows from Theorem 1.1. □ □

Auxiliary results and postponed proofs

Stationary number statistics cannot be deterministic

We prove the following lemma, for which we could not find a citable reference.

Lemma 10.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {Z}$$\end{document}Z be a stationary point process on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {C}$$\end{document}C with positive and finite first intensity. Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ {{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}\#\big (\mathcal {Z} \cap \overline{B}(0,R) \big )> 0, \qquad \text{ for } \text{ all } R>0. $$\end{document}Var#(Z∩B¯(0,R))>0,forallR>0.

Proof

Suppose on the contrary that there exists R>0R>0 with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Var}\,}}\big (\mathcal {Z} \cap \overline{B}(0,R) \big )=0$$\end{document}Var(Z∩B¯(0,R))=0. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N:= \mathbb {E} \big [\#\big (\mathcal {Z} \cap \overline{B}(0,R) \big )\big ]$$\end{document}N:=E[#(Z∩B¯(0,R))]. Thus, for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta \in \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}ζ∈C, the event \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\#\big [\mathcal {Z} \cap \overline{B}(\zeta ,R) \big ]= N$$\end{document}#[Z∩B¯(ζ,R)]=N has probability one. In particular, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N \in \mathbb {N} \cup \{0,\infty \}$$\end{document}N∈N∪{0,∞}, while by the assumption of the first intensity, 0<N<∞ 0<N<∞. Collecting exceptional events for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta \in \mathbb {Q} + i \mathbb {Q}$$\end{document}ζ∈Q+iQ we find an event \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _0$$\end{document}Ω0 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {P}(\Omega _0)=1$$\end{document}P(Ω0)=1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\#\big [\mathcal {Z} \cap \overline{B}(\zeta ,R) \big ]= N$$\end{document}#[Z∩B¯(ζ,R)]=N for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta \in \mathbb {Q} + i \mathbb {Q}$$\end{document}ζ∈Q+iQ.

Fix a realization Λ Λ of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {Z}$$\end{document}Z within the event \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _0$$\end{document}Ω0, so that10.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \#\big [\Lambda \cap \overline{B}(\zeta ,R) \big ]= N, \qquad \zeta \in \mathbb {Q} + i \mathbb {Q}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}#[Λ∩B¯(ζ,R)]=N,ζ∈Q+iQ.Since N \ge 1N≥1, there exists at least one point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \in \Lambda $$\end{document}λ∈Λ. Consider the set of exceptional points\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Gamma = \bigcup _{\lambda '\in \Lambda \setminus \{\lambda \}} \left( \partial B(\lambda ,R) \cap \partial B(\lambda ',R)\right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Γ=⋃λ′∈Λ\{λ}∂B(λ,R)∩∂B(λ′,R).By (10.1), Λ Λ has finitely many points on any given compact set. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial B(\lambda ,R) \cap \partial B(\lambda ',R)$$\end{document}∂B(λ,R)∩∂B(λ′,R) has at most two points, it follows that Γ Γ is finite. We can therefore choose a point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z \in \partial B (\lambda ,R) \setminus \Gamma $$\end{document}z∈∂B(λ,R)\Γ. This choice of z guarantees that10.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial B(z,R) \cap \Lambda = \{\lambda \}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂B(z,R)∩Λ={λ}.Since Λ Λ is locally finite, the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{B}(\zeta ,R) \cap \big ( \Lambda \setminus \{\lambda \} \big )$$\end{document}B¯(ζ,R)∩(Λ\{λ}) remains unaltered for all centers ζ ζ that are sufficiently close to z. Thus (10.2) allows us to choose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta _1, \zeta _2 \in \mathbb {Q} + i \mathbb {Q}$$\end{document}ζ1,ζ2∈Q+iQ, two small perturbations of z, such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \# \big [ \Lambda \cap \overline{B}(\zeta _1, R) \big ] < \big [ \Lambda \cap \overline{B}(\zeta _2, R) \big ], \end{aligned}$$\end{document}#[Λ∩B¯(ζ1,R)]<[Λ∩B¯(ζ2,R)],by simply including or excluding λ λ from the observation disk. This contradicts (10.1), and completes the proof. □ □

Convolution estimates

Lemma 10.2

There exists a constant C>0C>0 such that10.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sup _{z \in \mathbb {C}} \int _{w:|w| = R} (1+|z-w|)^{-2} \, |dw| \le C. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}supz∈C∫w:|w|=R(1+|z-w|)-2|dw|≤C.

Proof

By applying a rotation, we assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z=a \in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}z=a∈R. We also assume that R\ge 4R≥4. Note first that10.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{\begin{array}{c} w:|w| = R,\\ |w-a| > \sqrt{R} \end{array}} (1+|w-a|)^{-2} \, |dw| \le 2\pi (1+\sqrt{R})^{-2} R \lesssim 1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫w:|w|=R,|w-a|>R(1+|w-a|)-2|dw|≤2π(1+R)-2R≲1.On the other hand, writing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w=Re^{i\theta }$$\end{document}w=Reiθ with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \in (-\pi ,\pi ]$$\end{document}θ∈(-π,π], we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|w-a| \ge R |\sin (\theta )|$$\end{document}|w-a|≥R|sin(θ)|. Hence, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|w-a| \le \sqrt{R}$$\end{document}|w-a|≤R, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\sin (\theta )| \le R^{-1/2} < 1/2$$\end{document}|sin(θ)|≤R-1/2<1/2 and thus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\theta | \le c |\sin (\theta )|$$\end{document}|θ|≤c|sin(θ)| for an absolute constant c>0c>0. Therefore \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|w-a| \ge \tfrac{R}{c} |\theta |$$\end{document}|w-a|≥Rc|θ|, while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\theta | \le c R^{-1/2}$$\end{document}|θ|≤cR-1/2. With this information, we estimate10.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{\begin{array}{c} w:|w| = R,\\ |w-a| \le \sqrt{R} \end{array}} (1+|w-a|)^{-2} \, |dw|&\le R \int _{-cR^{-1/2}}^{cR^{-1/2}} \big (1+|\tfrac{R}{c}\theta |\big )^{-2}\, d\theta \nonumber \\&\lesssim \int _{-\sqrt{R}}^{\sqrt{R}} (1+|t|)^{-2}\,dt \lesssim 1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫w:|w|=R,|w-a|≤R(1+|w-a|)-2|dw|≤R∫-cR-1/2cR-1/2(1+|Rcθ|)-2dθ≲∫-RR(1+|t|)-2dt≲1.Combining (10.4) and (10.5) we obtain (10.3). □ □

Lemma 10.3

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le \delta < 1$$\end{document}0≤δ<1. Then there exists a constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_\delta $$\end{document}Cδ such that for all R,L>0R,L>0:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sup _{z:|z|=R} \int _{w:|w|=R, |z-w| \le L} |z-w|^{-\delta }\, |dw| \le C_\delta L^{1-\delta }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}supz:|z|=R∫w:|w|=R,|z-w|≤L|z-w|-δ|dw|≤CδL1-δ.

Proof

By applying a rotation, we assume that z=Rz=R, and by rescaling, we assume that R=1R=1. Assume first that L łe 1/2L≤1/2, write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w=e^{i\theta }$$\end{document}w=eiθ with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \in (-\pi ,\pi ]$$\end{document}θ∈(-π,π], and note that if |w-1| łe L|w-1|≤L, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\sin (\theta )| \le L < 1/2$$\end{document}|sin(θ)|≤L<1/2, so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\theta | \le c |\sin (\theta )|$$\end{document}|θ|≤c|sin(θ)| for an absolute constant c>0c>0. As a consequence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|w-1| \ge |\sin (\theta )| \ge |\theta |/c$$\end{document}|w-1|≥|sin(θ)|≥|θ|/c, and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{\begin{array}{c} w:|w|=1,\\ |w-1| \le L \end{array}} |w-1|^{-\delta }\, |dw| \le \int _{-{cL}}^{{cL}} \big (\tfrac{|\theta |}{c}\big )^{-\delta }\,d\theta \le C_\delta L^{1-\delta }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫w:|w|=1,|w-1|≤L|w-1|-δ|dw|≤∫-cLcL(|θ|c)-δdθ≤CδL1-δ.Finally, if L \ge 1/2L≥1/2, the previous case gives\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{\begin{array}{c} w:|w|=1,\\ |w-1| \le 1/2 \end{array}} |w-1|^{-\delta }\, |dw|\le C_\delta \le C'_\delta L^{1-\delta }, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫w:|w|=1,|w-1|≤1/2|w-1|-δ|dw|≤Cδ≤Cδ′L1-δ,while\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{\begin{array}{c} w:|w|=1,\\ |w-1| \ge 1/2 \end{array}} |w-1|^{-\delta }\, |dw| \lesssim C_\delta \lesssim C'_\delta L^{1-\delta }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫w:|w|=1,|w-1|≥1/2|w-1|-δ|dw|≲Cδ≲Cδ′L1-δ.□ □

Lemma 10.4

Let 0<p<20<p<2. Then there exists a constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_p>0$$\end{document}Cp>0 such that10.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{\mathbb {C}} |z+w|^{-p} e^{-|z|^2} \, dA(z) \le C_p (1+|w|)^{-p}, \qquad w \in \mathbb {C}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫C|z+w|-pe-|z|2dA(z)≤Cp(1+|w|)-p,w∈C.

Proof

First note that, by the Hardy-Littlewood rearrangement inequality,10.7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{\mathbb {C}} |z+w|^{-p} e^{-|z|^2} \, dA(z) \le \int _{\mathbb {C}} |z|^{-p} e^{-|z|^2} \, dA(z) < \infty , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫C|z+w|-pe-|z|2dA(z)≤∫C|z|-pe-|z|2dA(z)<∞,while10.8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{|z+w|\ge |w|/2} |z+w|^{-p} e^{-|z|^2} \, dA(z) \le 2^p |w|^{-p} \int _{\mathbb {C}} e^{-|z|^2} \, dA(z) = C_p |w|^{-p}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫|z+w|≥|w|/2|z+w|-pe-|z|2dA(z)≤2p|w|-p∫Ce-|z|2dA(z)=Cp|w|-p.Second, if |z+w| łe |w|/2|z+w|≤|w|/2, then |w| łe 2|z||w|≤2|z| and10.9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{|z+w| \le |w|/2} |z+w|^{-p} e^{-|z|^2} \, dA(z)&\le e^{-|w|^2/4} \int _{|u|\le |w|/2} |u|^{-p} \, dA(u)\nonumber \\&\le C'_p e^{-|w|^2/4} |w|^{2-p}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫|z+w|≤|w|/2|z+w|-pe-|z|2dA(z)≤e-|w|2/4∫|u|≤|w|/2|u|-pdA(u)≤Cp′e-|w|2/4|w|2-p.Hence, (10.7) gives (10.6) for |w| łe 1|w|≤1, while (10.8) and (10.9) cover the case |w| >1|w|>1. □ □

The planar chaos decomposition is independent of the choice of basis

Recall the notation of Sect. 7.2. As we now argue, the chaos decomposition (7.7) is independent of the choice of the orthonormal basis of G. While this is implicit in [37], we offer the following short argument.

We further split chaotic subspaces as follows. Given a polynomial p in the variables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _1, \overline{\eta _1}$$\end{document}η1,η1¯, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ldots \,$$\end{document}…, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _k, \overline{\eta _k}$$\end{document}ηk,ηk¯, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _1, \ldots , \eta _k \in G$$\end{document}η1,…,ηk∈G, we say that p is of degree łe (M,N)≤(M,N) if it is of total degree łe M≤M in variables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _1,\ldots , \eta _k$$\end{document}η1,…,ηk and of total degree łe N≤N in variables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\eta _1},\ldots , \overline{\eta _k}.$$\end{document}η1¯,…,ηk¯. We consider the polynomial spaces\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{Pol}_{M,N}(G)&= \text {span} \{ p(\eta _{1}, \overline{\eta _1}, \ldots , \eta _{k}, \overline{\eta _k}): p \text { polynomial of degree} \\&\qquad \qquad \qquad \le (M,N), \eta _1, \ldots , \eta _k \in G \}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}PolM,N(G)=span{p(η1,η1¯,…,ηk,ηk¯):ppolynomial of degree≤(M,N),η1,…,ηk∈G}.These spaces split the chaotic spaces as follows.

Proposition 10.5

For a (circularly symmetric) Gaussian random function F with probability space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Omega ,\mathbb {P})$$\end{document}(Ω,P), the following decomposition holds:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C_{M,N}= \overline{\textrm{Pol}_{M,N}(G)} \ominus \overline{\textrm{Pol}_{M-1,N}(G)} \ominus \overline{\textrm{Pol}_{M,N-1}(G)}, \qquad M,N \ge 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}CM,N=PolM,N(G)¯⊖PolM-1,N(G)¯⊖PolM,N-1(G)¯,M,N≥0,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Pol}_{M,N}(G)=0$$\end{document}PolM,N(G)=0 if N<0N<0 or M<0M<0.

As a consequence, the spaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{M,N}$$\end{document}CM,N are independent of the choice of orthonormal basis of the Gaussian space G.

Proof

Let us consider, more generally, polynomial spaces associated with a (not necessarily closed) linear subspace of G. If V is a linear subspace of G, we let\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{Pol}_{M,N}(V)&= \text {span} \{ p(\eta _{1}, \overline{\eta _1}, \ldots , \eta _{k}, \overline{\eta _k}): p \text { polynomial of degree }\\&\qquad \qquad \qquad \le (M,N), \eta _1, \ldots , \eta _k \in V \}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}PolM,N(V)=span{p(η1,η1¯,…,ηk,ηk¯):ppolynomial of degree≤(M,N),η1,…,ηk∈V}.Let V be the linear span (i.e., finite linear combinations) of the variables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\xi _j: j \ge 0\}$$\end{document}{ξj:j≥0}. Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \overline{\textrm{Pol}_{M,N}(G)} = \overline{\textrm{Pol}_{M,N}(V)}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}PolM,N(G)¯=PolM,N(V)¯,while\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{Pol}_{M,N}(V)= \textrm{span} \bigg \{ \prod _{k=0}^\infty H_{\alpha _k, \beta _k}( \xi _k, \bar{\xi }_k): |\alpha | \le M, |\beta | \le N \bigg \}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}PolM,N(V)=span{∏k=0∞Hαk,βk(ξk,ξ¯k):|α|≤M,|β|≤N}.It follows from orthogonality of complex Hermite polynomials that the random variables\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \bigg \{ \prod _{k=0}^\infty H_{\alpha _k, \beta _k}( \xi _k, \bar{\xi }_k): |\alpha | =M, |\beta | = N \bigg \} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}{∏k=0∞Hαk,βk(ξk,ξ¯k):|α|=M,|β|=N}are orthogonal to the spaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Pol}_{M-1,N}(V)$$\end{document}PolM-1,N(V) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Pol}_{M,N-1}(V)$$\end{document}PolM,N-1(V), and, therefore, also to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Pol}_{M-1,N}(G)$$\end{document}PolM-1,N(G) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Pol}_{M,N-1}(G)$$\end{document}PolM,N-1(G). Consequently,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\overline{\textrm{span}} \bigg \{ \prod _{k=0}^\infty H_{\alpha _k, \beta _k}( \xi _k, \bar{\xi }_k): |\alpha | =M, |\beta | = N \bigg \}\\&\qquad = \overline{\textrm{Pol}_{M,N}(G) }\ominus \overline{\textrm{Pol}_{M-1,N}(G)}\ominus \overline{\textrm{Pol}_{M,N-1}(G)}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}span¯{∏k=0∞Hαk,βk(ξk,ξ¯k):|α|=M,|β|=N}=PolM,N(G)¯⊖PolM-1,N(G)¯⊖PolM,N-1(G)¯,which proves the claim. □ □

Proof of Lemma 7.1
